- a single decision episode is differentiated into proposal, objection, qualification, delayed uptake, repair, and closure-placement units;
- one documented Non-Event window is differentiated into successive expectation windows, delays, blocks, and repeated non-decisions;
- one Path segment is proposed for differentiation into transitions, subpaths, branch points, and temporally bounded interruptions;
- one coarse Asymmetry claim is proposed for differentiation into role, access, cost, timing, exposure, and exit relations;
- one Frame-typed occurrence is proposed for differentiation into the practices through which relevance boundaries are reproduced.

These examples identify possible **relation forms**. They do not establish that the finer structures exist as described, that the sources support them, that they are functional components, or that the coarser claim will survive. Those are later burdens.

#### Granularity is a relation, not a volume measure

A reconstruction is not finer merely because it contains:

- more words;
- more quotations;
- more observations;
- more timestamps;
- more files;
- a larger diagram;
- a longer code representation;
- or more named details.

Added material may leave the operative distinction set unchanged. A transcript can become longer while still treating the entire review as one decision episode. A timeline can contain more dates while still omitting the transitions that would matter to a Path claim. A role table can contain more names while retaining the same undifferentiated access relation. Data volume and resolution are therefore separate.

```text
added description
≠ changed granularity
```

A genuine granularity change must answer at least four preparatory questions:

1. Which source distinction is presently compressed?
2. Which target distinction is proposed?
3. Along which dimension is the target finer?
4. What bounded claim-relevant difference could the new distinction test?

Source route, comparability, loss, and operation outcome remain necessary later, but these four questions establish whether a coherent coordinate proposal exists at all.

#### The same reference relation must remain explicit

SUB does not authorize analysts to replace a difficult source object with a more convenient object and call the result finer resolution. If the reference changes, that change must be acknowledged as a new analytical relation or a new claim.

For example, opening a recorded project-review conversation into utterance-and-repair units may preserve the same historical conversation as reference object. Replacing the conversation with a general organizational culture claim does not. The latter may be a legitimate later analysis, but it is not a finer reconstruction of the same object merely because the culture claim uses more abstract or more extensive material.

```text
new object described in greater detail
≠ finer reconstruction of the original object
```

The reference-preservation burden is not satisfied by repeating the same label. The source and target analyses must remain traceably related to the same bounded historical or analytical object. If the proposed target resolution fragments the source reference beyond recognition, combines several source objects without declaration, or silently changes the claim predicate, the claimed granularity relation is not established.

#### Granularity may be multi-dimensional

Granularity is not assumed to form one universal scalar from coarse to fine. A reconstruction may become finer along one dimension while remaining stable or becoming coarser along another.

A project review may be reconstructed:

- more finely in temporal segmentation;
- at the same institutional scope;
- more coarsely in individual role differentiation;
- and under an unchanged decision-coordination Frame.

Likewise, a Trajectory may be opened into shorter temporal segments while several local actor positions are compressed into role families. It would be inaccurate to describe the whole reconstruction as simply “finer” without declaring the relevant dimension.

```text
finer along dimension A
≠ finer along every dimension
```

Where several dimensions change independently, the analysis should preserve several local granularity relations rather than force them into a single hierarchy. Where no coherent comparison dimension can be stated, the relation remains underdetermined. A universal micro–meso–macro ladder is neither required nor authorized.

#### No truth, causality, completeness, or authority descent

A finer resolution can improve discrimination for a bounded claim. It can show that a coarse event contains temporally distinct episodes, that a stable Frame is reproduced by heterogeneous practices, or that one aggregate cost claim distributes unevenly across roles. But this possible gain does not grant finer resolution a general epistemic privilege.

A finer reconstruction may also:

- preserve the coarser result without adding relevant discrimination;
- obscure relations that are visible only at the coarser resolution;
- fragment a process whose larger temporal order is constitutive;
- overextend the available sources;
- substitute speculation for unresolved structure;
- create false precision;
- or become incomparable to the claim it was meant to test.

The Chapter-19 rule is therefore strict:

```text
finer granularity
≠ deeper reality
≠ higher truth
≠ stronger causality
≠ greater completeness
≠ additional authority
```

A more discriminating reconstruction for one claim can be less adequate for another. The question is not whether the target is “closer to reality” in the abstract, but whether the changed distinction set produces source-supported praxeological purchase while preserving traceable relation to the source object and claim.

#### C19-TRUTH-01 — finer proposal without truth descent

A review transcript is first reconstructed at decision-sequence resolution. A finer proposal distinguishes interruptions, qualifications, delayed uptake, repair attempts, and closure placement. The proposal may improve the analysis of whether an objection was rejected, deferred, absorbed, or left unresolved.

The finer proposal does not establish that utterance-level reconstruction is the true level of the meeting. It does not invalidate every decision-sequence claim, reveal final constituents, establish causal primacy, or authorize stronger institutional conclusions. Its warrant remains bounded to the declared question, sources, Frame, and comparison dimension.

The correct claim is:

```text
utterance-and-repair resolution may discriminate this decision claim more precisely
```

The incorrect claim is:

```text
the utterance level reveals what the meeting really was
```

The first prepares a testable granularity relation. The second converts resolution into ontology and authority.

#### Granularity declaration is not operation identity

Even a well-formed source-to-target resolution proposal does not establish that `DECOMPOSE` has occurred. Chapter 19 defines the coordinate relation required by a possible decomposition. Chapter 20 must still determine whether:

- the target distinctions are source-supported;
- relevant components and relations can be reconstructed;
- the same reference object remains intact;
- the current/coarser function can be tested;
- the operation remains within the Admissibility Band;
- and a canonical result can be assigned.

```text
granularity change proposed
≠ DECOMPOSE executed
```

This separation prevents coordinate language from becoming a substitute for analytical work.

<a id="19-2-relative-downward-movement"></a>

### 19.2 Relative Downward Movement

SUB is often described as moving “downward.” Within STRATA, this expression is permitted only as a relational shorthand. It indicates movement from an object treated as one occurrence or composite at a source position toward a proposed reconstruction of finer internal, reproductive, temporal, relational, or distributed structures. It does not refer to fixed lower layers of reality.

A relative downward movement may describe:

- a composite opened toward constituent or disturbing relations;
- an occurrence opened toward the practices and conditions through which it is reproduced;
- a Path opened toward transitions, subpaths, branch points, delays, and interruptions;
- a Trajectory opened toward segments, carriers, sedimentation routes, and changed action corridors;
- a structured Non-Event opened toward expectation windows, repeated non-realizations, blocks, and temporal costs;
- a Frame-typed occurrence opened toward source-supported inclusion, exclusion, routing, and repetition practices.

The direction is analytical. It does not establish smaller physical objects, more fundamental causes, lower operator rank, final constituents, or a universal micro-level.

```text
relative downward movement
≠ ontological descent
```

#### Comparator burden

Every statement that one analytical position is “below,” “inside,” “more local,” or “more granular” than another requires four declarations:

```text
object
+ comparator
+ relation
+ purpose
```

**Object** identifies what is being positioned.  
**Comparator** identifies the other analytical position relative to which the directional term is used.  
**Relation** states the changed distinction dimension or composition relation.  
**Purpose** states the bounded claim for which the directional comparison matters.

Without these declarations, “downward” is non-operational language. A conversation is not inherently micro, a quarterly process is not inherently macro, and an utterance is not universally lower than an institution. Each position is relative to a declared comparison relation.

#### C19-DOWN-01 — downward movement with declared comparator

A Trajectory is currently represented as three temporally ordered phases: initial coordination, repeated deferral, and stabilized closure. The proposed finer analysis distinguishes the repeated-deferral phase into documented expectation windows, delayed responses, responsibility-routing episodes, and abandoned repair attempts.

The downward claim is valid only in the bounded form:

```text
source object:
repeated-deferral phase within the declared Trajectory

comparator:
phase-level representation

relation:
proposal to distinguish source-supported internal temporal and routing structures

purpose:
test whether the phase-level Λ and Ω claims remain warranted
```

Nothing in this declaration proves that expectation windows are ontologically lower entities, that they are more causal, or that the Trajectory is reducible to them. The phrase “downward movement” names the analytical relation between the phase-level source representation and the proposed finer distinction set.

#### Granularity and relative level remain separate

Granularity concerns the resolution of distinctions. Relative level concerns the position of an object within a declared composition relation. These coordinates can change together, but neither entails the other.

A finer granularity can occur while relative level remains stable. The same project-review conversation may remain the relevant composite relative to the same utterance-and-response family while the analysis distinguishes repairs, deferrals, and closure placement more finely.

A relative-level change can occur while granularity remains stable. The same conversation can be repositioned as one constituent of a quarterly governance process while retaining its decision-sequence resolution.

```text
finer granularity
≠ changed relative level automatically

changed relative level
≠ finer granularity automatically
```

This distinction matters because an unmarked level shift can silently change the claim. A local interaction reconstructed more finely does not thereby become evidence for a higher-level institutional function. Conversely, placing a local object within a larger composite does not by itself reveal more of its internal structure.

#### C19-LEVEL-01 — finer resolution without changed relative level

The recorded project-review conversation is treated as one composite relative to its utterances, responses, and repair attempts. The source analysis distinguishes decision-sequence units. The target proposal distinguishes utterance, interruption, qualification, delayed uptake, repair, and closure-placement units.

The relative-level relation remains:

```text
conversation composite
relative to
utterance-and-response structures
```

What changes is the distinction density within that same declared relation. The conversation does not become a lower-level object, a higher-level object, or a new target function merely because its internal temporal and interactional resolution becomes finer.

The case therefore supports:

```text
changed granularity
+ stable relative level
```

It rejects:

```text
changed granularity
→ automatic level change
```

#### Downward movement does not determine parthood

Chapter 19 does not yet define the full criteria for functional components. That duty belongs to Sections 19.5–19.7. At this stage, relative downward language identifies only a proposed direction of analytical opening. It does not allow every smaller datum, spatially contained item, temporally earlier event, or named detail to count as a part of the source function.

The later component test must remain capable of finding that a proposed detail is:

- source-relevant and carrying;
- source-relevant and disturbing;
- replaceable;
- merely descriptive;
- unsupported;
- or unrelated to the coarser claim.

Thus:

```text
downward direction declared
≠ component status established
```

<a id="19-3-stable-frame-finer-granularity"></a>

### 19.3 Stable Frame, Finer Granularity

The typical SUB coordinate case preserves the analytical Frame and reference object while changing the distinction set. This configuration allows a later finer reconstruction to test the same bounded predicate under a more discriminating resolution.

The stable-Frame case requires more than reusing the same Frame label. The source and target analyses must preserve sufficiently stable:

- relevance rules;
- included and excluded material;
- claim predicate;
- source reference;
- temporal scope;
- and transformation purpose.

If those conditions change materially, the analysis may be making a new Frame-bound claim rather than testing the previous claim at finer resolution.

#### The prepared running case

Consider a recorded project-review conversation.

The source analysis declares:

```text
reference object:
recorded project-review conversation

Frame:
decision coordination

source granularity:
decision-sequence units

current/coarser claim under test:
the objection was rejected within the review process
```

The target proposal retains the same conversation and decision-coordination Frame but proposes a finer distinction set:

```text
proposed target resolution:
utterance
interruption
qualification
delayed uptake
repair attempt
closure placement
```

The expected praxeological difference is declared before the finer reconstruction:

```text
test whether the coarse rejection claim should remain,
be qualified as deferral,
be reconstructed as partial uptake,
or remain underdetermined
```

This expected difference does not predict the result. It specifies why the proposed distinction set could matter and what bounded claim is under pressure.

```text
expected difference declared first
≠ target result predetermined
```

The source bundle, temporal scope, and claim family remain fixed. Chapter 20 must later determine whether the transcript and associated records support the proposed units, how they relate, and what they do to the coarse rejection claim.

#### C19-GRAN-01 — same source and Frame, genuine distinction-set change

In the source representation, the meeting record contains one coarse sequence:

```text
objection raised
→ discussion
→ decision continued
```

The target proposal does not merely add quotations. It distinguishes structures that the source representation treated together:

```text
objection formulation
→ chair interruption
→ qualification by the objector
→ delayed uptake by another role
→ attempted repair
→ closure placement outside the current decision window
```

The reference object remains the same recorded conversation. The Frame remains decision coordination. The temporal scope remains the bounded review episode. The claim predicate remains whether and how the objection entered the decision process.

The granularity relation is genuine because the target proposal changes which interactional and temporal differences are represented separately. It is not established merely by greater textual detail.

The case remains preparatory. It does not yet prove that each proposed unit is source-supported, that they form components of the same source function, or that the coarse rejection claim fails.

#### Frame stability is a substantive continuity claim

Frame preservation cannot be inferred from terminology alone. A source analysis and target analysis may both use the phrase “decision coordination” while applying different relevance rules. One may treat only formal votes as decisions; the other may treat agenda placement, silence, routing, and delayed uptake as decision-bearing structures. If this difference changes the tested predicate, the Frame is not simply stable by declaration.

A stable-Frame claim therefore requires a visible continuity statement:

- what remains relevant in both analyses;
- which exclusions remain in force;
- which predicate is being tested;
- and how the finer distinctions remain subordinate to that same bounded question.

Frame stability is sufficient for coordinate continuity only where the source and target analyses remain comparable in this sense. Full comparability criteria remain owned by Section 19.8.

#### A stable Frame does not guarantee a valid decomposition

Even where reference and Frame remain stable, the proposed finer resolution may fail. The sources may not support the target distinctions. The added distinctions may not alter the warranted reconstruction. The finer analysis may destroy the visibility of the overall decision sequence. The source object may turn out to combine several processes that cannot responsibly be represented through one target relation.

Thus:

```text
stable Frame
+ proposed finer granularity
≠ admissible DECOMPOSE automatically
```

The coordinate relation is necessary but not sufficient. Chapter 20 retains procedure and result ownership; Chapter 25 retains resolution classification.

<a id="19-4-changed-frame-during-decomposition"></a>

### 19.4 Changed Frame during Decomposition

A Frame change may accompany finer analysis, but it must be separately declared. It cannot be hidden inside the language of increased resolution, and it is not by itself evidence that `DECOMPOSE` has occurred.

Four situations must remain distinct:

1. **Stable Frame, finer granularity** — the same bounded predicate is tested under a changed distinction set.
2. **Internal subframe during finer reconstruction** — a bounded internal relevance rule is introduced while source lineage and the relation to the source claim remain explicit.
3. **Different analytical Frame** — the same historical material is reconstructed under a new relevance rule and therefore carries a new Frame-bound claim.
4. **Target-context function claim** — the source object is projected to perform a bounded function in another context, potentially requiring later `PROJECT_AS` rather than SUB alone.

The differences are not terminological. They determine which claim is being made and which operation, if any, is later appropriate.

#### The contrast case

The same recorded project-review conversation can be reconstructed under two Frames:

```text
Frame A:
decision coordination

Frame B:
responsibility allocation
```

Both analyses may use utterance-and-repair units. The distinction density can therefore remain approximately stable while the Frame changes.

Under decision coordination, the question is:

> How did the objection enter, fail to enter, or remain pending in the decision sequence?

Under responsibility allocation, the question is:

> Which role-position received, could answer, displaced, retained, or redistributed responsibility for the objection?

The second question is not a finer version of the first merely because it examines the same transcript closely. It selects different relevance relations and supports a new Frame-bound claim.

```text
same material
+ similar resolution
+ changed relevance rule
→ changed Frame-bound claim
```

#### C19-FRAME-01 — accompanying Frame change declared separately

Suppose a later decomposition of a coarse organizational Asymmetry claim requires a bounded access-allocation subframe. The source Frame concerns decision coordination across the review. The internal subframe isolates how speaking order, agenda control, document access, and closure authority distribute entry into the decision process.

The analysis must state:

- that an internal Frame has been introduced;
- why it is needed for the finer question;
- how it remains related to the original source reference;
- which source claim remains under test;
- and which new Frame-bound claim is not inherited from the source analysis.

The accompanying Frame change may be admissible. It does not disappear merely because the target resolution is finer, and it does not inherit the result or authority of the source Frame.

```text
finer analysis with declared internal Frame
≠ unchanged-Frame decomposition
```

#### C19-FRAMEONLY-01 — Frame change alone is not DECOMPOSE

The project-review conversation is reconstructed first under decision coordination and then under responsibility allocation. Both reconstructions use the same utterance-and-repair resolution. No source distinction is divided further in the second analysis; only the relevance rule and tested predicate change.

This is a legitimate new Frame-bound analysis of the same historical material. It is not `DECOMPOSE` merely because the second account appears more revealing or focuses on relations that were previously backgrounded.

```text
Frame change alone
≠ granularity change
≠ DECOMPOSE
```

Calling the second analysis a decomposition would erase the new claim boundary and permit it to inherit support from the first Frame. The correct response is to record a changed Frame and new testable claim. A later decomposition may occur within either Frame, but it requires its own declared source-to-target granularity relation and operation record.

#### Internal subframe versus substituted object

An internal subframe remains connected to the same source object only where the source-reference relation is traceable. If an analysis of access allocation leaves the recorded review and begins making claims about the organization’s full governance culture, the source object and temporal scope have expanded. That may warrant a new analysis, a new `COMPOSE` construction, or another source declaration. It cannot be treated as an internal finer view of the original episode without explicit support and transformation records.

Likewise, a Frame change that assigns the source object a function in another target context may cross into RETYPE. Chapter 19 marks this risk but does not adjudicate the complete SUB/RETYPE boundary; Chapter 26 retains that ownership.

#### Frame changes do not erase prior claims or failures

A new Frame can expose a different structure, but it does not retroactively repair a failed source claim. If the decision-coordination analysis fails to establish that the objection was rejected, a responsibility-allocation analysis cannot rescue that claim by changing the question. It creates a new testable claim.

```text
new Frame
= new bounded claim
≠ repaired prior claim automatically
```

The same rule applies to Stop and Non-Capture. A Mandatory Stop under one proposed granularity relation remains part of the record. A later Frame may support another admissible inquiry, but it does not remove the source limitation that stopped the earlier one.

#### WP1 handoff

Sections 19.1–19.4 establish:

- granularity change as source-to-target distinction-set change;
- multi-dimensional comparison without a universal scale;
- finer resolution without truth, causal, completeness, or authority descent;
- relative downward movement without ontology;
- granularity and relative level as separate coordinates;
- the stable-Frame positive case;
- the separately declared Frame-change contrast;
- expected praxeological difference before operation execution;
- and the prohibition on identifying `DECOMPOSE` from coordinate change alone.

They do not yet establish:

- the full criteria for a functional component;
- local versus distributed component forms;
- component necessity or sufficiency;
- comparability status;
- the Minimal Granularity Relation as a completed declaration;
- source support for actual target structures;
- component relations;
- source-function effect;
- `DECOMPOSE` result;
- or a Chapter-25 resolution classification.

The controlled continuation is:

```text
Sections 19.1–19.4
→ Sections 19.5–19.7: distinction set, distributed structure, component criteria
→ Sections 19.8–19.11: comparability, mismatch, Lower Granularity Question, Minimal Granularity Relation
→ Chapter 20: DECOMPOSE procedure and result
```

The local WP1 status remains:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

This status records a bounded canonical production step under an open Chapter 19 and open SUB Part. It does not classify any decomposition occurrence, target granularity, component set, source-function effect, or resolution outcome.

<a id="19-5-change-of-distinction-set"></a>

### 19.5 Change of Distinction Set

A finer granularity must change the distinctions through which the bounded source reference is reconstructed. It must not merely enlarge the description of an unchanged partition. The target proposal therefore needs to state which source-side differences were previously compressed, which target-side differences are now to be represented separately, and how those differences could alter the warranted reconstruction under the declared Frame.

A **distinction set** includes more than a list of labels. It includes:

- which units are represented separately;
- which relations among those units are represented;
- which temporal orderings, overlaps, thresholds, or gaps become visible;
- which roles, costs, exposures, accesses, expectations, and Non-Events are differentiated;
- and which differences remain compressed or excluded.

Changing the names of existing units does not necessarily change the distinction set. Adding quotations to an already identified episode does not necessarily change it. Nor does dividing a transcript into equal-length segments, listing every participant, or assigning more timestamps automatically produce a praxeologically finer reconstruction. The new distinction must alter how the source object can be tested as praxis structure.

```text
more description
≠ more distinctions

more distinctions
≠ praxeologically relevant distinctions automatically
```

#### Candidate distinction families

Depending on the source object and claim, a proposed target resolution may distinguish:

- role-positions with different response capacities;
- access, exposure, burden, exit, and documentation differences;
- event, sub-event, Transition, and interruption relations;
- expectation windows, delays, blocks, omissions, and repeated non-realizations;
- local reproduction practices and recurrent maintenance conditions;
- branch points, parallel subpaths, deferred continuations, and lost alternatives;
- temporal thresholds, durations, overlaps, and order effects;
- or local and distributed carrier relations.

This list is not an ontology of internal parts. It does not imply that every occurrence or composite contains all of these structures, or that the proposed distinctions are source-supported. It identifies possible forms of finer discrimination. The actual warrant remains bounded by the Chapter-18 source declaration, the source basis, the Frame, the expected praxeological difference, and the later Chapter-20 procedure.

#### Distinctions may concern relations, not only smaller units

Finer reconstruction is often misdescribed as dividing one object into smaller objects. Some granularity changes instead differentiate relations that were previously compressed.

A coarse review analysis may already identify the same speakers and statements found in the target proposal. The target becomes finer only when it distinguishes, for example:

- who could introduce an issue and who could only respond;
- which intervention interrupted, qualified, deferred, or redirected another;
- which silence occurred inside an active expectation window;
- which role controlled closure placement;
- or how access to records changed the later interpretability of the episode.

No new spatial fragment is required. The increased resolution lies in the differentiated relational structure.

```text
finer reconstruction
≠ smaller spatial pieces only
```

Likewise, one source event can be opened through temporal relations without assuming that the event was falsely identified. A documented decision can remain a valid coarse event while the target proposal distinguishes proposal, challenge, delayed uptake, procedural closure, and later implementation. The source event remains the reference point; the target distinction set tests what the event claim compresses and whether that compression remains warranted.

#### Every added distinction needs a claim relation

A proposed distinction is not chapter-relevant merely because it is available in the source. It must identify a bounded reconstruction that it could alter, qualify, pressure, or leave unchanged.

For the running review case, the target proposal distinguishes:

```text
objection formulation
chair interruption
qualification
later uptake
repair attempt
closure placement
```

The claim relation is not “these details are interesting.” It is:

```text
test whether the coarse claim of rejection
should remain,
be qualified as deferral,
be reconstructed as partial uptake,
or remain underdetermined
```

This relation keeps the target distinctions connected to the source claim without predicting the result. If the distinctions cannot affect the bounded reconstruction, they may remain accurate descriptive material but do not yet establish a warranted finer granularity for the present inquiry.

#### Selection remains visible

A changed distinction set is still selective. It does not recover every difference inside the source object. Distinguishing interruptions and delayed uptake may still compress prosody, document circulation, prior role histories, or later implementation. Chapter 19 therefore cannot present the target proposal as complete simply because it is finer along the declared dimension.

The selection burden is:

- why these distinctions are proposed;
- why they matter to the current claim;
- which other distinction dimensions remain stable or compressed;
- and which source limitations constrain the target proposal.

Full operation-level Loss remains owned by Chapter 20. Chapter 19 nevertheless protects the fact that granularity change redistributes visibility rather than eliminating compression.

#### C19-DIST-01 — actual distinction-set change versus added description

Two analysts extend the same review transcript analysis.

The first adds five quotations, two participant biographies, and a more detailed narrative of the meeting. The analysis still treats the objection, discussion, and continued decision as the same three undifferentiated units. The operative distinction set has not changed.

The second distinguishes interruption, qualification, delayed uptake, repair attempt, and closure placement, and relates each distinction to the bounded rejection-versus-deferral claim. This is a genuine granularity proposal because previously compressed interactional and temporal differences become separately testable.

The second proposal is not automatically admissible or correct. The sources may fail to support some distinctions, or the distinctions may leave the warranted reconstruction unchanged. But it crosses the Chapter-19 threshold that the first account does not:

```text
added material without changed partition or relation
→ no established granularity change

new source-related distinctions with a bounded claim relation
→ coherent granularity proposal
```

The case also blocks a false numerical test. Five new distinctions are not necessarily better than two. The relevant question is whether the changed distinction set creates source-supported praxeological discrimination within the Admissibility Band.

<a id="19-6-local-versus-distributed-structure"></a>

### 19.6 Local versus Distributed Structure

A finer reconstruction must not assume that every source-relevant component occupies one place, one moment, one document, or one role. A provisionally compressed object can be carried, modified, or disturbed by structures that are locally concentrated, temporally distributed, institutionally distributed, role-distributed, relationally constituted, or recurrent across non-contiguous occurrences.

This is an anti-atomistic rule. It does not deny local components. It denies that locality, smallness, or spatial containment is the general criterion of component status.

```text
part of the source structure
≠ spatially contained fragment automatically
```

#### Locally concentrated structure

A locally concentrated candidate is identifiable within a bounded source segment or site and has a plausible relation to the source claim.

In the review case, the chair's explicit closure statement may be a locally concentrated candidate. It occurs at a delimited point in the transcript and may bear directly on whether the objection was procedurally closed, deferred, or redirected.

Local concentration does not by itself make the statement a functional component. The statement may be ceremonial, contradicted by later routing, or irrelevant to the bounded claim. Its component status depends on source support, relation to the source object, praxeological relevance, and relation to other proposed structures.

#### C19-LOCAL-01 — locally concentrated component candidate

A recorded closure statement reads: “We will proceed with the current plan and revisit the objection next month.” The statement is a plausible local candidate for the decision-coordination reconstruction because it may differentiate rejection from deferral and places the issue in a later temporal window.

The candidate is locally concentrated, source-visible, and claim-related. It is not yet treated as sufficient proof that deferral occurred. Later records may show that no revisit mechanism existed, that the statement lacked procedural force, or that the objection was separately absorbed into implementation. Chapter 19 therefore admits the local candidate without assigning its final component status or source-function effect.

```text
locally identifiable candidate
≠ sufficient component
≠ decisive cause
```

#### Temporally distributed structure

Some structures exist only across ordered or repeated occurrences. One apparently punctual Non-Event may be reconstructed through several expectation windows, delays, responsibility handoffs, and missed opportunities. No single moment contains the whole structure.

For example, “the promised review did not occur” may be too coarse if the source supports:

```text
review promised
→ materials not circulated
→ responsibility transferred
→ meeting delayed
→ agenda slot omitted
→ response window expired
```

The distributed structure is not a collection of unrelated absences. Its component relation depends on the declared expectation Frame, temporal order, and the way the repeated non-realizations alter costs, alternatives, or later reachability.

#### Role-distributed and relational structure

A coarse Asymmetry claim may be carried across several role-positions:

- one role controls agenda admission;
- another controls document circulation;
- another bears response obligations;
- another carries reputational exposure;
- another possesses formal exit but faces disproportionate practical cost.

No single role difference need be necessary or sufficient for the coarse Asymmetry. The finer reconstruction may need to represent how the relations reinforce, offset, or interrupt one another.

Relationally constituted components are especially important. An access asymmetry is not always a property located in one actor. It may exist in the relation between authorization rules, document availability, timing, and role-specific response capacity. Removing the relation and listing only the actors would destroy the structure that the decomposition is meant to test.

#### Institutional distribution

A Frame-typed occurrence can be reproduced across sites that do not appear together in one episode. Agenda rules may be maintained in one document, access permissions in another system, speaking order in meeting practice, record authority in minutes approval, and closure procedures in later routing.

The finer target proposal may therefore cross several source locations while preserving one bounded reference object or arrangement. This does not automatically expand the analysis into an institution-wide claim. The source relation, temporal scope, and Frame must remain explicit. Where the source object expands, a new source declaration or operation may be required.

#### Distributed does not mean macro

A distributed component is not automatically a higher-level object. Distribution describes how the proposed finer structure is carried; it does not determine relative level by itself.

A decision-access relation distributed across three roles and two procedural sites may still be part of a finer reconstruction of one bounded review arrangement. Conversely, a locally concentrated event may function as a composite relative to its own internal interactional units. Locality, distribution, granularity, and relative level therefore remain separate coordinates.

```text
distributed structure
≠ macro-level structure automatically

local structure
≠ micro-level primitive automatically
```

#### C19-DISTR-01 — distributed candidate without spatial atomism

The review record shows no single act that excludes an objection. Instead:

- agenda admission is controlled before the meeting;
- supporting documents reach one role late;
- the objection is interrupted during discussion;
- responsibility for follow-up is assigned without access authority;
- the minutes record closure but omit the pending qualification;
- and the promised revisit window expires.

The candidate structure is distributed across time, roles, documents, and procedural sites. Treating only the interruption as “the component” would compress the relation that the finer analysis is meant to inspect.

Chapter 19 permits the distributed candidate because it remains traceably related to the bounded review arrangement and the decision-coordination claim. It does not yet establish that all listed elements form one component, that they causally produce the outcome, or that a coarse Asymmetry or Frame claim is confirmed. Chapter 20 must reconstruct the actual relations and source support.

<a id="19-7-functional-parts-versus-mere-fragments"></a>

### 19.7 Functional Parts versus Mere Fragments

Not every accurate detail within or around a source object is a functional component of the reconstruction. A detail becomes a **component candidate** only where it has a declared, source-supported, and praxeologically relevant relation to the bounded source object and to the coarser function or claim under test.

The Chapter-19 component test is conjunctive. A proposed component candidate must have:

1. **reference relation** — a traceable relation to the same bounded source object;
2. **source route** — direct support or an explicitly bounded route through the declared source basis;
3. **function/claim relation** — a plausible carrying, modifying, disturbing, constraining, or testing relation to the coarser function or claim;
4. **praxeological relevance** — capacity to alter the warranted reconstruction of roles, costs, access, expectations, transitions, alternatives, bindings, or another declared praxis dimension;
5. **relational placement** — a stated relation to other proposed components or to the internal organization of the source object.

Failure of any one element prevents the detail from being treated as an established functional component at the Chapter-19 gate. The detail may remain evidentially accurate, contextually useful, or worthy of another analysis. It is not thereby a component of the present decomposition claim.

```text
accurate detail
≠ component automatically

component candidate
≠ established component

participation
≠ necessity
≠ sufficiency
≠ causal priority
```

#### Carrying components

A carrying component contributes to the reproduction, maintenance, or constitution of the coarser source function. It may help explain how a Frame boundary is reproduced, how an Attractor persists, how an Asymmetry is distributed, or how a Trajectory carries historical load.

Carrying does not mean that the component is indispensable on its own. Several different practices may substitute for one another while the coarser function persists. Nor does carrying establish that the component is the principal cause of the source structure. The relation can be partial, conditional, redundant, or context-dependent.

#### C19-COMP-01 — carrying component without necessity or sufficiency inference

In the review arrangement, agenda control is a plausible carrying component of the decision-coordination Frame. It helps determine which objections become procedurally visible and when they can enter the formal sequence.

The analysis does not infer:

- that agenda control alone creates the Frame;
- that the Frame would disappear if one agenda practice changed;
- that agenda control is the deepest or primary cause;
- or that every instance of agenda control carries the same function.

Other practices—document circulation, speaking-order rules, closure authority, minutes approval, or recurring Non-Events—may carry overlapping parts of the same Frame function. The component claim therefore remains bounded:

```text
agenda control contributes to the reproduction of this bounded Frame occurrence
```

It does not become:

```text
agenda control is necessary and sufficient for the Frame
```

Counterfactual necessity, sufficiency, substitution, and component overelasticity remain later burdens.

#### Disturbing components

A component can be source-relevant because it weakens, interrupts, contradicts, or destabilizes the coarser function. Source relation does not require preservation of the source claim.

A coarse review analysis may describe a stable decision Frame. A finer source-supported repair sequence may show that participants repeatedly suspend, contest, or reverse the closure rule. If those disturbances materially alter the warranted reconstruction, they must remain visible even where they undermine the coarse claim.

```text
component relevance
≠ support for the coarse function
```

The preservation requirement from Chapter 18 protects the source function as a test target, not as a mandatory result. Chapter 19 therefore admits disturbing candidates on equal methodological terms with carrying candidates.

#### C19-DISRUPT-01 — disturbing component retains rejection power

The meeting minutes present a coherent closure: the current plan was approved and the objection was scheduled for later review. The transcript, however, contains a repeated sequence in which participants dispute who has authority to defer the issue, reopen the agenda after the stated closure, and leave the follow-up role without access to the required documents.

This sequence is a plausible disturbing component because it may show that procedural closure did not produce a stable decision-coordination function. The sequence may require the coarse claim to be qualified, reduced, or rejected.

The analysis may not relabel the sequence as “noise” merely to preserve the minutes-based account. Nor may it conclude immediately that the coarse claim fails. Chapter 19 preserves the disturbance as a component candidate with genuine counterpressure; Chapter 20 must reconstruct its relations and source-function effect.

#### Replaceable and redundant components

Some components participate in a source function while remaining replaceable. A meeting Frame may persist when one moderator changes because agenda templates, minutes procedures, access rules, and role expectations reproduce comparable effects. A recurring delay structure may persist through different handoff failures. A distributed Asymmetry may remain while one local cost relation is removed.

Replaceability matters because a decomposition that treats every participant as necessary will overstate internal dependence. Conversely, the existence of substitutes does not make the component irrelevant. The correct claim can be:

```text
this component contributes under the current configuration,
while alternative carriers may preserve the coarser function
```

Chapter 19 identifies this possibility. Chapter 20 and later Counterfactual Component Tests own any stronger conclusion.

#### Mere fragments

A fragment is a detail severed from the relational and claim burden required for component status. It may be small, vivid, precise, or source-authentic while remaining analytically non-constitutive for the present question.

A proposed fragment fails where it:

- lacks a traceable relation to the bounded source object;
- is selected only because it is small, dramatic, or easy to quote;
- cannot alter the current warranted reconstruction;
- lacks adequate source support;
- is disconnected from other internal relations;
- changes the topic or Frame rather than the granularity;
- or is assigned necessity, sufficiency, or causal force without evidence.

#### C19-FRAG-01 — accurate detail rejected as component

The review transcript records that one participant arrived seven minutes late and used a red notebook. Both details are accurate. Neither is connected, under the available sources, to agenda admission, objection uptake, closure placement, access, cost, responsibility, or the bounded rejection-versus-deferral claim.

Adding the details increases descriptive density. It does not establish a functional component or a finer praxeological reconstruction.

If later evidence showed that late admission systematically prevented access to pre-meeting documents, the timing detail could acquire a source-function relation under a separately supported claim. Under the current record, however, it remains a fragment.

```text
source-authentic detail
+ no bounded function/claim relation
→ fragment for this reconstruction
```

The rejection is local, not absolute. STRATA does not declare the detail meaningless in every possible Frame. It declares only that the detail does not meet the current conjunctive component test.

#### Anti-atomism and anti-causal-overclaim

The component test blocks two opposite errors.

**Atomistic error:** the analysis treats the smallest visible details as the real parts of the source object and assumes that a complete list will reconstruct the whole.

**Causal-overclaim error:** the analysis identifies one source-relevant component and promotes it to necessary, sufficient, primary, or exhaustive cause without testing substitutions, interactions, temporal order, and alternative models.

A valid finer proposal may require both local and distributed candidates, relations among them, and explicit uncertainty. The resulting structure need not be reducible to one privileged component or one causal hierarchy.

```text
functional component
≠ final constituent
≠ isolated atom
≠ automatically causal component
```

#### WP2 handoff

Sections 19.5–19.7 establish:

- changed distinction set rather than added description;
- relation-level as well as unit-level refinement;
- a bounded claim relation for each proposed distinction;
- local, temporal, relational, institutional, and role-distributed component forms;
- distribution without automatic macro-level status;
- the conjunctive component-versus-fragment test;
- carrying, disturbing, and replaceable component possibilities;
- source support and relational placement burdens;
- and the rejection of necessity, sufficiency, causal priority, or final-constituent inference from participation alone.

They do not yet establish:

- actual source-supported components for a completed `DECOMPOSE` occurrence;
- final component relations;
- component necessity, sufficiency, or counterfactual indispensability;
- the source-function effect;
- comparison between competing finer reconstructions;
- a comparability status;
- granularity mismatch or substantive contradiction;
- the Lower Granularity Question as an evaluated test;
- the populated Minimal Granularity Relation;
- `DECOMPOSE` admissibility or result;
- or a Chapter-25 resolution classification.

The controlled continuation is:

```text
Sections 19.5–19.7
→ Sections 19.8–19.11: comparability, mismatch, Lower Granularity Question, Minimal Granularity Relation
→ Chapter 20: component/relation reconstruction and DECOMPOSE result
```

The local WP2 status remains:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

This status records a bounded canonical production step. It does not certify any target component, distributed structure, causal relation, source-function effect, operation result, or resolution outcome.

<a id="19-8-granularity-comparability"></a>

### 19.8 Granularity Comparability

Two finer reconstructions are not comparable merely because they concern the same named object, contain similar amounts of detail, or are both described as “micro.” Comparability is a declared relation between bounded claims. It depends on whether the reconstructions preserve enough common reference, Frame, predicate, temporal scope, source standard, and granularity dimension for one result to bear on the other without silent substitution.

The relevant question is not:

> Which reconstruction is finer?

It is:

> Under what declared basis can these reconstructions test the same bounded claim, and what translation or loss is required for the comparison?

A comparison may therefore be local even where both reconstructions address the same source object. One pair of claims may be comparable with respect to decision authority but not with respect to interactional repair. Two temporal partitions may align for one transition and diverge for another. A source-supported relation at one segment does not establish whole-object comparability.

```text
same named object
≠ comparable reconstruction automatically

same amount of detail
≠ same granularity

comparability for one predicate
≠ comparability for every predicate
```

#### The comparability basis

A responsible comparison declares at least:

1. **reference basis** — whether the same source reference, or an explicitly related source reference, remains in view;
2. **predicate basis** — which bounded claim is being compared;
3. **Frame basis** — whether the same relevance rule is operative or whether a translation between Frames is declared;
4. **temporal basis** — whether the reconstructions cover sufficiently aligned periods, windows, transitions, or analytical cuts;
5. **source-standard basis** — whether the source routes, access conditions, uncertainty standards, and version states are sufficiently compatible;
6. **granularity-dimension basis** — along which distinction dimension the source and target positions are compared;
7. **translation basis** — how unlike distinction sets are related, including what cannot be translated;
8. **loss basis** — what each reconstruction preserves, compresses, excludes, leaves uncertain, or renders irrecoverable.

These burdens are conjunctive for the predicate under comparison. A shared historical reference cannot compensate for incompatible predicates. A shared predicate cannot compensate for unrelated temporal scopes. A translation rule cannot compensate for unsupported source units. More detailed source material cannot repair a broken reference relation.

#### Local comparison descriptions

Chapter 19 permits four local comparison descriptions. They are analytical descriptions, not new canonical Output Classes and not a closed global enum.

**Comparable.** The reconstructions test the same bounded predicate under sufficiently aligned reference, Frame, temporal scope, source standard, and granularity dimension. Differences in representation remain traceable and do not erase constitutive structure.

**Comparable with declared translation.** The reconstructions use different but translatable distinction sets. The translation rule, residual mismatch, and loss are explicit. Translation does not make the representations identical or mutually substitutable.

**Partially comparable.** Some predicates, segments, roles, or relations align while others do not. The comparison must be segmented. A local comparison result may not be generalized to the whole source object.

**Incomparable or underdetermined.** The current Frames, predicates, temporal scopes, source standards, or distinction dimensions do not support a responsible comparison. The correct result is to withhold the comparison rather than manufacture equivalence or conflict.

```text
comparable
≠ identical
≠ lossless translation
≠ mutually substitutable

incomparable
≠ both claims true
≠ neither claim testable elsewhere
≠ permanent impossibility of comparison
```

The descriptions remain revisable. Additional source access, a better translation relation, a narrower predicate, or a corrected temporal alignment may change the local comparison status. Conversely, a comparison that initially appears straightforward may become only partial once excluded relations or source asymmetries are disclosed.

#### Translation without identity

Translation is required where the reconstructions partition the source object differently but still address a common predicate. For example, one reconstruction may distinguish the review conversation into interactional units—proposal, interruption, qualification, repair, uptake, closure—while another distinguishes it into procedural stages—agenda admission, deliberation, authority exercise, documentation, follow-up. These distinction sets are not identical.

A declared translation may state that:

- interactional closure placement bears on the procedural closure stage;
- delayed uptake bears on follow-up status;
- authority challenge bears on authority exercise;
- repair attempts bear on whether procedural closure integrated the objection;
- some interactional details have no procedural analogue;
- some document-routing relations have no utterance-level analogue.

The comparison is possible only for the bounded predicate that survives this mapping. The translation cannot erase unmatched structures or convert every local difference into a procedural difference.

#### C19-COMPARE-01 — comparable reconstructions under a declared basis

Two analyses reconstruct the same bounded review arrangement and ask whether the objection was procedurally integrated before the recorded closure.

The first analysis uses interactional units from the transcript:

- objection entry;
- interruption;
- qualification;
- proposed repair;
- delayed uptake;
- closure statement.

The second uses procedural units from the agenda, minutes, and routing record:

- agenda admission;
- deliberation stage;
- authority placement;
- closure recording;
- follow-up assignment.

The source reference, decision-coordination Frame, review window, and predicate are aligned. A declared translation relates the repair and uptake units to the procedural question of integration before closure. The translation also records that tone, turn length, and several local interruptions do not independently map to procedural integration, while document-access timing is visible only in the procedural source route.

The reconstructions are therefore comparable with declared translation for the bounded integration predicate. They are not identical, lossless, or globally interchangeable. Neither is ranked as the truer level.

#### Positive incomparability

Incomparability is a bounded methodological finding. It prevents unsupported ranking and false contradiction. It can arise where:

- one reconstruction concerns a different historical reference;
- the Frames make different structures relevant;
- the temporal windows do not overlap responsibly;
- one source route supports interactional sequence while another supports only later institutional aggregation;
- the predicates differ without a valid translation;
- the target granularities vary along unrelated dimensions;
- or the available sources cannot establish the relation.

A positive incomparability finding must name the failed relation. “The analyses are different” is insufficient. The record should state whether the problem lies in reference, Frame, predicate, time, source standard, dimension, translation, or loss.

#### C19-INCOMP-01 — positive incomparability without plural-truth inference

One reconstruction examines the single review conversation under a decision-coordination Frame and asks how authority, objection uptake, and closure were organized during that meeting. A second reconstruction aggregates twelve months of project delays under a delivery-performance Frame and asks which process stages recurrently exceeded planned duration.

The two analyses concern related institutional material, but they do not preserve the same bounded reference, temporal scope, predicate, or granularity dimension. The meeting analysis cannot be translated into a yearly process-duration claim without additional composition and source work. The yearly aggregate cannot decide how the objection was treated in the meeting.

The correct local description is incomparability for the present comparison. This does not establish that both claims are true, that neither can be tested, or that the objects are unrelated. It establishes only that no responsible direct comparison has yet been declared.

#### Comparability and authority

A successful comparison does not increase authority. It may show that one reconstruction is better calibrated for a bounded predicate, that one claim requires reduction, or that two claims can coexist. It does not authorize person judgment, causal certainty, institutional verdict, or universal scale ranking.

Likewise, incomparability does not protect a weak claim. A coarse claim can fail on its own source basis even when no finer rival is comparable. A finer claim can be unsupported even when the coarse claim is also weak. Comparison status and claim validity remain separate.

<a id="19-9-granularity-mismatch"></a>

### 19.9 Granularity Mismatch

A **granularity mismatch** occurs when reconstructions use non-aligned distinction sets and are treated as though they directly affirm or deny the same bounded predicate. The mismatch can produce an apparent disagreement where the claims actually concern different structures. It can also conceal a genuine contradiction by allowing one claim to escape into a different scale label.

The first task is therefore not to decide which granularity wins. It is to reconstruct the claim relation:

```text
reference
+ Frame
+ predicate
+ temporal scope
+ source basis
+ granularity dimension
→ relation between claims
```

Only after that relation is explicit can the analysis distinguish:

- compatible claims about different predicates;
- partially overlapping claims;
- claims requiring translation;
- incomparable claims;
- and genuinely contradictory claims.

#### Apparent conflict through different predicates

A coarse and a fine reconstruction may both be warranted where they answer different bounded questions.

For example:

- “The review reached documented procedural closure” concerns the existence of a recorded closure under a procedural predicate.
- “Several repair attempts remained interactionally unresolved before closure” concerns whether the interaction integrated those repairs before the closure statement.

The first claim does not entail complete interactional resolution. The second does not deny that a closure was recorded. Their coexistence is not secured merely by calling one macro and the other micro. It is secured by showing that the predicates differ and are compatible within the same bounded reference.

#### C19-MISMATCH-01 — apparent conflict explained by bounded predicates

The minutes state that the current plan was approved and the objection was moved to a later review. A finer transcript reconstruction shows that the proposed repair was never accepted, rejected, or assigned a source-supported revisit condition before the closing statement.

The coarse claim is:

```text
procedural closure was documented
```

The fine claim is:

```text
interactional repair remained unresolved at the moment of closure
```

The two claims can coexist because “documented procedural closure” and “completed interactional repair” are different predicates. The finer analysis qualifies what the closure accomplished; it does not erase the recorded event. The coarse analysis identifies an event; it does not establish integration of every unresolved structure.

The apparent conflict is therefore a granularity-and-predicate mismatch. The resolution depends on explicit predicate separation, not on an automatic micro/macro harmony rule.

#### Substantive contradiction remains possible

Different granularities do not immunize claims from contradiction. A genuine contradiction remains where the reconstructions preserve the same reference, Frame, temporal scope, and predicate, and one affirms what the other denies.

For instance:

- Claim A: the identified role-position possessed and exercised final decision authority in the bounded review conversation.
- Claim B: under the same role definition, review window, and source basis, the role-position neither possessed the authorization nor performed the decision act.

These claims cannot both be maintained merely because one was produced from a coarse minutes-based analysis and the other from a finer transcript-and-policy reconstruction. The granularity difference may explain how the contradiction arose. It does not dissolve the contradiction.

#### C19-CONFLICT-01 — genuine contradiction preserved across granularities

A coarse reconstruction of the minutes assigns final decision authority to the project lead and states that the lead approved the plan. A finer reconstruction checks the same meeting, the same authority predicate, the same decision point, the governing authorization record, and the transcript.

The finer source basis shows:

- the project lead was authorized to recommend, not decide;
- the formal decision role was assigned elsewhere;
- no statement by the project lead performed the final decision act;
- the minutes attributed the approval after the meeting without a source-supported authorization event.

The claims address the same bounded reference and predicate. One affirms possession and exercise of final authority; the other denies both under aligned sources and definitions. This is a substantive contradiction, not a harmless granularity mismatch.

Chapter 19 preserves the contradiction. It does not yet assign the operation result or final Output Class. Chapter 20 must reconstruct the relevant components and relations; later outcome classification may require claim reduction, failure, Stop, or another canonical result. What is prohibited is the statement that both claims are correct because one is coarse and the other fine.

#### Invalid micro/macro neutralization

The following move is invalid:

```text
claim A and claim B conflict
→ A is macro and B is micro
→ both are correct
```

The scale labels do not establish different predicates, compatible scopes, or a translation relation. They may hide:

- a changed Frame;
- a changed source object;
- an equivocal term;
- a shifted temporal window;
- an unsupported generalization;
- or a direct contradiction.

The valid procedure is:

```text
identify the predicates
→ align reference, Frame, time, and source basis
→ declare the granularity relation
→ test compatibility, partial overlap, incomparability, or contradiction
```

#### Mismatch, claim reduction, and Non-Capture

A finer reconstruction may support a narrower coarse claim. For example, the source may support “closure was recorded” but not “the objection was integrated.” That pressure may later require `claim_reduction_required`. Chapter 19 identifies the relation and protects the narrower claim from being erased; it does not yet assign the class.

Non-Capture remains available where materially different source-supported partitions cannot be translated into one responsible relation. But Non-Capture cannot turn contradiction into plural truth, preserve an unsupported coarse claim, or grant equal standing to an unsourced finer model.

```text
mismatch identified
≠ contradiction resolved automatically

incomparability
≠ plural validity

new granularity
≠ failed prior claim erased
```

<a id="19-10-the-lower-granularity-question"></a>

### 19.10 The Lower Granularity Question

The central lower-boundary question for Chapter 19 is:

> Does the additional distinction change a warranted reconstruction of the praxis-relevant structure?

The question is deliberately claim-sensitive. It does not ask whether more detail exists, whether the target description is more vivid, or whether the analyst can partition the source object further. It asks whether the proposed distinction can alter a warranted reconstruction under the declared Frame, reference, source basis, and claim scope.

A positive answer requires more than a possible difference. It requires a plausible route by which the distinction could change the reconstruction of at least one relevant structure, such as:

- action corridors;
- access or exclusion;
- cost or exposure distribution;
- role or responsibility placement;
- event or Non-Event structure;
- temporal order, duration, delay, or irreversibility;
- branch availability;
- Frame reproduction;
- Attractor persistence;
- Asymmetry distribution;
- binding or exit conditions;
- or the status of the current/coarser function under test.

The question can produce four preparatory states:

1. **claim-relevant difference identified** — a source-supported distinction has a plausible route to changing the warranted reconstruction;
2. **difference proposed, support still open** — the expected praxeological difference is coherent, but the target source route or component relation remains unresolved;
3. **no claim-relevant difference stated** — the proposal adds description without showing what warranted reconstruction could change;
4. **responsible answer unavailable** — the coordinates, sources, or comparison relation do not permit a bounded judgment.

These are pre-operation states. They are not canonical Output Classes and do not decide Chapter-25 resolution outcomes.

#### No automatic gain

A valid finer reconstruction may leave the current warranted claim unchanged. It may reveal additional internal variation without affecting the predicate under test. It may also reduce calibration by fragmenting the source object, obscuring distributed relations, or making the comparison basis less stable.

The possibility of neutrality or deterioration is constitutive to SUB. Otherwise every successful partition would count as analytical gain, and the Relevance Floor would disappear.

```text
valid finer distinction
≠ additional praxis finding automatically

more internal variation visible
≠ current claim changed automatically
```

#### C19-LOWER-01 — no claim-relevant change without premature classification

A review conversation is reconstructed first at decision-sequence resolution and then at a finer utterance resolution. The finer version distinguishes greeting, seating comments, repeated acknowledgements, several pauses, and minor turn overlaps. The distinctions are source-supported and accurately timed.

For the bounded claim—whether the objection entered the decision sequence, received a supported response, and altered closure—the added units do not change the reconstruction. They reveal no new access relation, authority placement, repair sequence, delay, cost distribution, Non-Event, or alternative closure path.

Chapter 19 records that no claim-relevant difference has yet been shown. It does not assign `resolution_neutral`, resolution drift, or failure. Those classifications require the later operation and comparison architecture. The present result leaves those possibilities open and supports an Optional Stop if no further bounded reason is available.

#### Optional Stop

Optional Stop is warranted where:

- the current/coarser representation already carries the bounded claim;
- the proposed distinction lacks additional praxeological purchase;
- the target relation is unnecessary for the present work package;
- further partition would reduce calibration or comparability;
- or the same expected difference can be tested without executing a finer reconstruction.

Optional Stop is positive discipline. It does not assert that the object has no further structure or that no future decomposition could be useful under another claim.

#### Mandatory Stop

Mandatory Stop is required where continuation would depend on:

- unsupported target units;
- source-reference substitution;
- concealed Frame change;
- operator-type decomposition;
- ontological or authority claims about a “lower” level;
- fragment lists without relational placement;
- a comparison that erases incompatible predicates or temporal scopes;
- incomparability used to shield a failed claim;
- or source and claim ceilings exceeded by the proposed granularity.

Mandatory Stop preserves weaker warranted findings where available. It does not convert the attempted relation into a successful `DECOMPOSE` occurrence.

#### Non-Capture at the lower boundary

Non-Capture remains necessary where:

- several source-supported target partitions remain materially different and no responsible priority relation can be established;
- a distributed object cannot be represented through one target granularity without destroying constitutive relations;
- available sources cannot discriminate among competing finer models;
- or a universal micro–meso–macro assignment would erase the local comparison dimensions.

Non-Capture does not preserve an unsupported source claim, validate every partition, or establish permanent indecomposability. It marks the limit of the current transformation claim.

#### Non-compensation

No amount of detail can compensate for:

- missing PraxisPurchase;
- missing TraceableLoad or source support;
- broken Reference Continuity;
- Frame confusion;
- incompatible temporal scope;
- failed Type Integrity;
- absent comparison basis;
- or authority overreach.

Formal elegance, dense coding, precise timestamps, or a large component list may improve legibility. They cannot create a warranted granularity relation where the constitutive burdens fail.

<a id="19-11-minimal-granularity-relation"></a>

### 19.11 Minimal Granularity Relation

Chapter 19 closes with a readable declaration of the source-to-target coordinate relation. The **Minimal Granularity Relation** contains exactly eight fields:

```yaml
granularity_change:
source_granularity:
target_granularity:
distinction_added:
frame_preserved:
reference_preserved:
expected_praxeological_difference:
comparability_status:
```

The declaration is minimal, not sufficient by itself. It establishes whether a coherent target relation has been stated before Chapter 20 attempts component and relation reconstruction.

#### Field discipline

`granularity_change` describes how the operative distinction set changes. It should name the comparison dimension rather than repeat “coarse to fine.”

`source_granularity` records the Chapter-18 source resolution under which the object is presently treated as compressed.

`target_granularity` records the proposed finer resolution. It is a target proposal, not proof that the target units exist as supported components.

`distinction_added` names the new difference, relation, ordering, threshold, distribution, or internal heterogeneity to be tested. A list of additional details without a claim relation is insufficient.

`frame_preserved` states whether the same relevance rule remains operative. The value requires rationale; matching Frame labels do not prove preservation. Where the Frame changes, the new Frame-bound claim must be separately declared.

`reference_preserved` states whether the source and target remain traceably related to the same bounded reference object. Matching object names do not prove continuity.

`expected_praxeological_difference` states what warranted reconstruction could change if the target distinction is supported.

`comparability_status` gives a local, reasoned comparison description—comparable, comparable with declared translation, partially comparable, incomparable, or underdetermined—together with the basis and uncertainty. It is not a new canonical Output Class or globally closed enum.

#### C19-MINREL-01 — populated conceptual relation without operation result

The review arrangement can be prepared for Chapter 20 as follows:

```yaml
granularity_change: >-
  The review is proposed to move from one documented decision episode to
  temporally ordered interactional and procedural units relevant to objection
  entry, authority exercise, repair, closure, and follow-up.
source_granularity: >-
  One bounded review occurrence reconstructed at decision-sequence resolution.
target_granularity: >-
  Proposed utterance-repair and procedural-stage resolution within the same
  bounded review occurrence.
distinction_added: >-
  Distinguish objection entry, interruption, qualification, repair proposal,
  authority challenge, closure placement, follow-up assignment, and expiry of
  the revisit window.
frame_preserved: >-
  Yes for the decision-coordination claim; the same relevance rule concerning
  objection uptake, authority, and closure remains operative.
reference_preserved: >-
  Yes provisionally; transcript, agenda, minutes, and routing records remain
  traceably tied to the same review occurrence.
expected_praxeological_difference: >-
  The finer distinction set may change whether the recorded closure is
  reconstructed as integrated decision, deferred unresolved objection, or
  closure under distributed access and authority asymmetry.
comparability_status: >-
  Comparable with declared translation for the bounded decision-integration
  predicate; interactional and procedural units require an explicit mapping,
  and unmatched tone or document-routing details remain disclosed as loss or
  non-translation.
```

This declaration does not establish that the listed units are actual components, that their relations are source-supported, that the coarse function survives, that `DECOMPOSE` succeeds, or that any canonical Output Class applies. It supplies Chapter 20 with a controlled coordinate hypothesis.

#### Mapping to the Shared Transformation Record

The Minimal Granularity Relation is not a parallel schema. Its fields map to the existing Shared Transformation Record and controlled extension discipline:

| Conceptual field | Existing record carrier |
| --- | --- |
| `source_granularity` | `/source/granularity` |
| `target_granularity` | `/target/granularity` |
| source Frame | `/source/frame` |
| target Frame | `/target/frame` |
| source reference | `/source/reference_object` |
| target reference | `/target/reference_object` |
| expected difference | `/operation/expected_praxeological_difference` |
| `granularity_change` | operation justification, relation declaration, or controlled `extensions` payload |
| `distinction_added` | controlled `extensions` payload or later operation-specific declaration |
| `comparability_status` | controlled `extensions` payload plus claim/relation rationale |

A record-local extension must state:

- owner or source;
- control pointer;
- purpose;
- bounded payload;
- relation to required fields;
- and that it neither replaces the schema nor adds automatic semantic authority.

No schema change is required for WP3. A later model revision may promote a recurring field only if the corpus establishes a stable owner need and the closed inventories remain intact.

#### Boolean caution and uncertainty

The apparent yes/no fields are claims, not machine-inferred facts. `frame_preserved: true` or `reference_preserved: true` requires an explicit basis. Where preservation is partial, contested, or underdetermined, the rationale must state that uncertainty rather than force a clean Boolean conclusion in prose.

A false or uncertain preservation result does not automatically fail every possible analysis. It may indicate:

- a separately declared new Frame-bound claim;
- a changed source reference;
- a different operation chain;
- a reduced claim;
- Non-Capture;
- or Mandatory Stop for the attempted `DECOMPOSE` relation.

The operation identity cannot be inferred from the field value alone.

#### Loss and claim ceiling

The coordinate relation must anticipate bidirectional loss through the canonical structure:

```yaml
loss:
  preserved:
  compressed:
  excluded:
  uncertain:
  irrecoverable:
```

A finer target may preserve local sequence while compressing wider procedural continuity. A coarser source may preserve the overall function while compressing internal heterogeneity. Neither position is lossless, and the target claim may not exceed the supported target distinctions, retained reference, declared Frame, temporal scope, source basis, or current operation stage.

#### Chapter-20 handoff

Chapter 19 supplies Chapter 20 with:

```text
bounded source reference
+ source granularity
+ proposed target granularity
+ distinction-set change
+ Frame status
+ reference status
+ expected praxeological difference
+ local comparability declaration
```

Chapter 20 owns:

- the exact decomposition question;
- operation preconditions;
- actual component identification;
- component-relation reconstruction;
- internal temporality;
- source support and uncertainty;
- source-function testing;
- operation-level Loss;
- failure conditions;
- the `DECOMPOSE` Record;
- and local Output Class mapping.

```text
Chapter 18
→ source object and reason

Chapter 19
→ source-to-target granularity relation

Chapter 20
→ DECOMPOSE procedure and result
```

<a id="chapter-19-completion-boundary"></a>

#### Chapter-19 completion boundary

The integrated WP4 audit confirms Chapter 19 as canonically complete and provisionally locked because Sections 19.1–19.11 establish:

- granularity change as distinction-set change rather than added volume;
- multi-dimensional resolution without truth descent;
- relative downward movement without ontology;
- granularity/Frame/relative-level separation;
- stable-Frame and separately declared changed-Frame cases;
- local and distributed component-candidate forms;
- functional-component versus fragment criteria;
- carrying, disturbing, and replaceable candidates without necessity or causal inflation;
- local comparability descriptions and translation duties;
- positive incomparability without plural-truth inference;
- mismatch versus substantive contradiction;
