An Extended Event is one bounded Event whose identity depends on several temporally distributed phases and a common completion relation. It is not simply an Event that lasted a long time. Duration is compatible with waiting, inactivity, unrelated interruptions, or a sequence of independent acts. Extension becomes an Event property only where the phases remain linked to the same source reference and jointly bear on the claimed change.

#### C23-EXTEND-01 — one Extended Event is not duration, sequence, or plurality alone

A candidate Extended Event requires at least:

1. a supported beginning condition;
2. two or more internally distinguishable phases;
3. a relation connecting those phases to the same bounded change;
4. continuity or a supported resumption relation across interruptions;
5. a completion criterion that distinguishes completion from mere cessation;
6. counterpressure under which the object would split, cluster, or fail.

```text
long duration
≠ Extended Event automatically

ordered phases
≠ one Event automatically

same topic
≠ same Event automatically
```

For E-23, the source may support the following candidate phase structure:

```text
Phase 1 — admission
objection enters the authorized review channel

Phase 2 — preparation
relevant materials are stabilized for review

Phase 3 — deliberation
eligible roles evaluate the admitted objection

Phase 4 — disposition
an authorized vote or decision act selects the outcome

Phase 5 — completion
binding entry and required communication make the disposition operative
```

The common topic is not enough to unify these phases. Their unity depends on the supported relation that each phase contributes to one bounded transition from admitted objection to operative disposition. If the preparation phase concerns another objection, if the vote addresses a materially altered question, or if the communication announces a separately made later decision, the one-Event claim weakens.

Completion must be distinguished from the last observed activity. A meeting can end without the Event completing. A record can be created before the decision becomes operative. Communication can occur while an appeal condition leaves the disposition provisional. Conversely, an Event may complete even though later consequences and disputes continue. The completion criterion must be tied to the bounded source function rather than to the last timestamp available.

#### C23-PHASE-01 — phases, thresholds, role shifts, and completion relations

Finer reconstruction may identify internal phases without treating each phase as an independent Event. A phase becomes analytically relevant where it changes at least one supported relation concerning:

- eligibility or access;
- role capacity;
- available alternatives;
- threshold status;
- decision bindingness;
- temporal exposure or cost;
- completion;
- categorical preservation;
- uncertainty or Loss.

The phase map for E-23 may show that admission and disposition were separated by a role shift: the chair controlled entry, a review panel assessed the objection, and an approving office issued the binding disposition. This does not automatically create three Events. It establishes differentiated roles within the candidate Extended Event.

Likewise, thresholds must be tied to the source claim. Possible thresholds include:

- sufficient information for deliberation;
- required quorum;
- authorization to vote;
- closure declaration;
- record finalization;
- communication activating practical effect.

A threshold can be crossed gradually, contested, or source-uncertain. Chapter 23 must not turn every threshold indicator into an Event. The relevant question is whether the threshold alters the status of the same bounded Event or marks the beginning of another object.

Interruptions also require a continuity test. A recess, information request, technical failure, or scheduled delay may occur within one Extended Event where the source procedure, reference, and completion relation remain continuous. A substantial reframing, new decision authority, changed object, or expired procedural window may instead divide the candidate into separate Events or create a cluster. Resumption is a claim about preserved reference and relation, not merely about activity restarting.

The following contrast is controlling:

```text
internally differentiated Event
→ phases remain related to one bounded completion

several Events
→ each has its own bounded change or completion relation
```

An Extended Event may therefore contain local Events in ordinary language, but Chapter-23 categorization depends on analytical role. A formal notice, vote, and entry can be positive occurrences inside the wider source Event without each becoming an independent Event object for the current claim.

<a id="23-3-event-cluster"></a>

### 23.3 Event Cluster

An Event Cluster is a bounded temporal object in which several locally distinguishable Events remain related through a common decision object, coordinated formation, dependency, threshold, transition, or completion environment. The cluster preserves the local Event identities more strongly than an Extended Event does. It does not reduce them to phases of one Event, yet it does not treat them as an unrelated chronology.

A cluster claim must specify:

- the local Events;
- each local Event's bounded change;
- the relation connecting them;
- the cluster boundary;
- the common praxis-relevant question;
- whether order, overlap, dependence, or shared completion is material;
- what would make the cluster relation fail.

#### C23-CLUSTER-01 — finer cluster reconstruction versus silent new COMPOSE

A coarse Event claim may be revised as an Event Cluster through `DECOMPOSE` where the source was already treated as one bounded temporal object and finer evidence reveals several locally complete but internally related Events. In that route, the analyst opens the same source reference and revises its internal category.

For example, E-23 may prove better represented as:

```text
Event E-23a:
objection admitted into the formal review channel

Event E-23b:
review panel issues a recommendation

Event E-23c:
approving office adopts or rejects the recommendation

Event E-23d:
operative closure is communicated

cluster relation:
all four Events address the same admitted objection and jointly determine its formal disposition
```

This cluster reconstruction is admissible as a `DECOMPOSE` candidate only if E-23 was already a warranted bounded source object and the local Events remain traceable to that same reference and function.

By contrast, an analyst who independently selects a meeting, a later email, an unrelated approval act, and a subsequent implementation change and then declares them one Event Cluster has formed a new analytical object. That formation belongs to `COMPOSE` unless an independently warranted coarse source object already binds them.

```text
coarse Event opened into related local Events
→ possible DECOMPOSE

previously independent Events selected and related
→ new COMPOSE claim
```

The operation boundary cannot be repaired by retaining the old label. `DECOMPOSE` preserves or revises the same reference object. `COMPOSE` selects and forms a new object. A cluster may be a valid target of either route, but the Records, selection burdens, Loss, and claims differ.

An Event Cluster also remains distinct from a Path. A cluster may contain order and dependency while remaining bounded around one common transition or completion environment. A Path requires a realized sequence of configurations and transitions with broader continuation structure, branch relations, and altered action corridors. Chapter 24 owns the decomposition of Path and Trajectory objects; WP1 does not convert E-23 into one.

#### C23-COUNTER-01 — coarse Event category remains revisable

Finer evidence may produce several legitimate category effects:

```text
one apparently punctual Event
→ preserved as one Event with finer components

one apparently punctual Event
→ refined as one Extended Event

one coarse Event
→ internally differentiated as an Event Cluster

one coarse Event
→ split into several unrelated Events

one coarse Event
→ rejected because no bounded praxis-relevant change is supported

one coarse Event
→ underdetermined because rival boundaries remain comparably supported
```

No outcome has truth priority merely because it is finer. A single Event may remain the best reconstruction where local distinctions do not alter transition, role, threshold, completion, access, cost, alternatives, or Loss. A cluster may be required where several locally complete changes retain distinct practical effects. Rejection may be necessary where the coarse account conflated records, repeated descriptions, or administrative carriers with the Event itself.

WP1 does not select a final Source-Function Effect, prior-claim disposition, or canonical Output Class. It establishes that category preservation is an auditable result dimension rather than an assumption. The later integrated result must keep the local operation result, category effect, prior coarse claim, and Output Class separate.

<a id="23-4-event-inflation"></a>

### 23.4 Event Inflation

Event Inflation occurs when increased observation density is converted into Event density without sufficient praxeological purchase. The analyst can always divide a recording into smaller units: sentences, turns, clicks, document saves, cursor movements, status changes, sensor ticks, or timestamps. Chapter 23 does not deny those changes. It denies that their isolability alone makes them Event components or independent Events for the current reconstruction.

#### C23-INFLATE-01 — Event Inflation and the local stop rule

Inflation pressure is present where:

- every utterance or document edit is typed as an Event;
- one praxis change is counted repeatedly because several systems record it;
- phases are detached from the completion relation that made them relevant;
- minimal state differences receive independent Event status without changed praxis structure;
- observation tools determine the Event unit;
- finer division destroys the source Event's reference or action unity;
- additional distinctions do not alter transition, role, threshold, expectation, completion, cost, alternative, source category, uncertainty, or Loss.

The local stop rule is:

> Do not introduce another Event distinction unless it changes a source-supported reconstruction of the bounded temporal object's praxis-relevant structure.

This rule applies before the analyst reaches the smallest recordable interval. The stop point is relational and claim-bound, not technologically fixed. A second-by-second log may be necessary where order at that scale changes access, authorization, threshold crossing, or irreversible execution. The same resolution may be pure noise where the source claim concerns a decision completed over several days.

```text
observation density
≠ Event density

more timestamps
≠ more PraxisPurchase

finer division
≠ higher truth
```

A duplicated record is a common inflation source. A vote may appear in meeting minutes, an approval database, a notification system, and an audit log. Four records do not establish four Events. They may provide convergent evidence for one Event, describe different phases, or document separate carrier acts. Their relation must be reconstructed rather than counted.

Similarly, a long interaction can contain many speech turns without containing many independent Event objects. One question, clarification, objection, response, amendment, and vote may form phases of one deliberative Event, several locally complete Events in a cluster, or merely observable fragments. The appropriate unit depends on the transition and completion claims under test.

#### C23-UNIT-01 — the Event unit remains claim-, Frame-, source-, and transition-relevant

A valid Event unit must remain bounded by five relations:

1. **Claim relation** — the unit changes a warranted proposition under test;
2. **Frame relation** — the unit is relevant inside the declared analytical boundary;
3. **Source relation** — the unit and its temporal position are actually supported;
4. **Transition relation** — the unit bears on a praxis-relevant change, threshold, completion, or alternative;
5. **Reference relation** — the unit remains traceable to the same source Event or is explicitly declared as a separate Event.

No universal minimum Event exists. An Event can be brief or extended, singular or clustered, heavily documented or sparsely evidenced. What matters is not its metric size but its reconstructive function.

The Event unit must also resist instrumental bias. Different systems expose different granularities. Video may expose gesture-level change; a database may expose status transitions; minutes may expose agenda-level decisions; participant records may expose role handoffs. Chapter 23 may compare these sources, but it cannot allow the most detailed instrument to define the true Event scale by default.

Where a finer distinction fails the local stop rule, the analysis should preserve the coarser unit for the current claim. This does not prove that the finer material is meaningless. It may become relevant under another Frame, claim, or source object. Under the present `DECOMPOSE` occurrence, however, it remains below the Praxeological Relevance Floor.

#### WP1 completion boundary

Sections 23.1–23.4 now establish:

- independently warranted Event-like source entry;
- a declared and revisable Event category;
- beginning, completion, contextual margins, interruptions, and rival Event boundaries;
- components as claim-bearing temporal relations rather than isolated observations;
- Extended Event criteria beyond duration or sequence;
- phase, threshold, role-shift, interruption, resumption, and completion relations;
- Event Cluster reconstruction with explicit `DECOMPOSE`/`COMPOSE` separation;
- preservation, extension, clustering, splitting, rejection, and underdetermination as open category effects;
- Event Inflation and a local praxeological stop rule;
- a claim-, Frame-, source-, transition-, and reference-bound Event unit.

WP1 does not yet establish the full decomposition of Non-Events, preservation of `Λ` through positive sub-events, expectation support, missing-information boundaries, delay mechanisms, repeated non-decision, internal temporal order, multiple clocks, temporal drift taxonomy, Event/Non-Event confusion, the final four-axis result, Failure, Mandatory Stop, Non-Capture, the seventeen-field temporal Record view, Chapter-24 handoff, or Chapter-23 lock.

```text
WP1 complete
≠ Chapter 23 complete
≠ one Event category automatically confirmed
≠ Non-Event decomposition complete
≠ Path or Trajectory formed
≠ final Output Class selected
≠ Chapter 23 provisionally locked
≠ SUB provisionally locked
```

The Chapter-23 result therefore remains `admissible_but_provisional`. The next controlled step is WP2, §§23.5–23.8: Non-Event Decomposition, preservation of the Non-Event character, Delay Structure, and Repeated Non-Decision.

<a id="23-5-non-event-decomposition"></a>

### 23.5 Non-Event Decomposition

A Non-Event is not opened by replacing an absence with whatever positive activity happened nearby. It is opened by reconstructing the source-supported relations through which an expected structure failed to occur within a bounded Frame and temporal condition. The source object remains the non-realization under test. Meetings, transfers, requests, deferrals, communications, partial approvals, resource decisions, and procedural closures enter the decomposition only where they bear on that same expected structure.

Chapter 23 therefore begins Non-Event Decomposition with a stricter entry burden than ordinary source absence. The analyst must identify what was expected, why the expectation was warranted, when or under what completion condition realization was due, what evidence supports non-realization, and what praxis-relevant difference followed. Without this conjunctive burden, temporal detail cannot turn missingness into `Λ`.

#### C23-NONEVENT-01 — conjunctive Non-Event source entry

A source may enter Non-Event Decomposition only where all of the following are declared and source-supported:

1. **Expected structure** — the Event, decision, communication, repair, transition, or binding occurrence expected to take place;
2. **Expectation Frame** — the institutional, relational, procedural, or practice structure that made the expectation warranted;
3. **Expected window or completion condition** — the bounded interval, deadline, trigger, or criterion within which realization was relevant;
4. **Non-realization** — evidence that the expected structure did not occur within that boundary;
5. **Praxis difference** — a supported change in access, alternatives, cost, exposure, role, coordination, closure, expectation, or continuation produced by the non-realization;
6. **Source limit** — known gaps and the maximum claim the available materials can carry.

A bounded declaration for the Chapter-23 anchor can be stated as:

```yaml
non_event_decomposition_entry:
  reference: bounded review non-decision R-23
  source_category: Non-Event under test
  expected_structure: binding review decision
  expectation_frame:
    - agenda commitment to decide the admitted objection
    - assigned decision role
    - stated completion deadline
  expected_window: admission of the objection through expiry of the follow-up deadline
  supported_non_realization: no binding vote or equivalent disposition within the declared window
  praxis_difference:
    - objection remained unresolved
    - implementation authority remained unsettled
    - coordination and exposure costs continued
  known_gaps:
    - informal communications not fully available
    - intermediate role deliberations partially undocumented
  decomposition_question: which positive processes produced, stabilized, or failed to overcome the bounded non-realization?
```

The declaration does not yet prove one Extended Non-Event, repeated Non-Decision, or any particular delay mechanism. It makes the coarse `Λ` claim testable. If the agenda contained no commitment to decide, if the assigned role lacked decision authority, if the deadline was merely aspirational, or if a binding disposition occurred through another supported route, the source claim must change.

A legitimate expectation need not be a promise made by a person. It can be carried by a rule, assigned procedure, scheduled decision point, established repair obligation, formally opened process, or source-supported role relation. Conversely, social hope, retrospective preference, or analytical convenience is insufficient. Chapter 23 reconstructs an expectation structure; it does not manufacture one because the later absence appears important.

#### C23-EXPECT-01 — expectation support is independently testable

Expectation support must be tested separately from the positive processes found inside the candidate Non-Event. Numerous meetings, requests, transfers, and deadline references can show that an issue remained active without proving that a binding decision was due within the claimed window. The analyst must therefore ask:

- What source establishes the expected structure?
- Which role, rule, practice, or commitment carried the expectation?
- Was the expectation operative for this object and this interval?
- Was realization mandatory, conventionally expected, conditionally possible, or merely preferred?
- Which preconditions had to be satisfied before the expectation became active?
- Did a later Frame change suspend, replace, or narrow it?

The `Λ` claim may fail even where the positive chronology is well documented. For R-23, the record may show two meetings and a missed date, yet later disclose that the date governed only an information update rather than a binding decision. In that case, the proposed decision Non-Event is not preserved by the density of internal activity. The source may instead support an incomplete information process, a different expected structure, or only uncertainty.

```text
many positive processes
≠ warranted expectation automatically

missed date
≠ missed decision automatically

important absence
≠ Non-Event automatically
```

Failure of the expectation claim does not erase the positive Events. It revises the categorical object under test. A meeting remains a meeting; a request remains a request; a transfer remains a transfer. What fails is the claim that these structures formed the internal architecture of the specified Non-Event.

#### C23-MISSING-01 — missing information remains distinct from structured non-occurrence

Missing records, unknown intermediate phases, silent files, incomplete minutes, or unavailable communications may establish uncertainty and Source Ceiling. They do not establish non-occurrence by themselves.

```text
no record of a decision
≠ decision did not occur

unknown intermediate phase
≠ delay established

mere absence in the archive
≠ structured Non-Event
```

A supported Non-Event may rely on positive evidence of non-realization: an open-status record after the deadline, a formal deferral, an explicit statement that no vote occurred, a later document noting unresolved status, or the continued absence of an operative disposition where the system would necessarily register one. Even then, the inference must remain bounded. The absence of a required entry may strongly support non-realization within that procedure while leaving open an external or informal action outside the declared Frame.

Where sources cannot distinguish non-occurrence from unrecorded occurrence, the correct result remains uncertainty, claim reduction, or later Non-Capture—not a stronger `Λ` narrative. Source absence cannot inherit the semantic load of the expected structure.

<a id="23-6-preserving-the-non-event-character"></a>

### 23.6 Preserving the Non-Event Character

Non-Event Decomposition may reveal extensive positive activity. That discovery does not create a categorical contradiction. The higher-level object concerns the failure of one expected structure to occur, while the finer reconstruction concerns the processes through which that failure was produced, stabilized, deferred, fragmented, or left unresolved.

#### C23-LAMBDA-01 — positive sub-events may carry the higher-level Non-Event

The Non-Event character is preserved where six relations remain supported:

1. the same expected structure remains identifiable;
2. the Expectation Frame remains warranted;
3. the expected window or completion condition remains bounded;
4. the expected structure did not occur within that boundary;
5. the positive sub-events bear a supported relation to producing, stabilizing, deferring, fragmenting, or failing to overcome that non-realization;
6. the non-realization produces a praxis-relevant difference.

For R-23, a finer map may contain:

```text
first meeting
→ decision deferred pending additional information

information request
→ readiness threshold remains unmet

responsibility transfer
→ access and timing conditions change

second meeting
→ discussion occurs without binding vote

follow-up deadline expires
→ bounded decision window closes without disposition
```

Each line describes positive occurrences. Together they may support one bounded Non-Event: the expected binding review decision did not occur within the operative window. The decomposition does not say that “nothing happened.” It says that what happened did not realize the specific expected structure and may have organized its non-realization.

This preservation test prevents two opposite errors. The first treats `Λ` as empty space and therefore refuses to reconstruct its internal activity. The second discovers positive activity and concludes that the Non-Event vanished. Both errors lose the relation between expectation and realization.

```text
positive sub-events present
≠ expected structure realized

internal activity reconstructed
≠ Non-Event dissolved

Non-Event preserved
≠ every positive sub-event causally sufficient
```

The relation between a positive sub-event and the Non-Event can vary. A request may directly defer readiness. A role transfer may alter access without itself causing the failure. A meeting may preserve procedural continuation while failing to cross the binding threshold. A communication may stabilize ambiguity. Chapter 23 must represent these differences rather than flattening all sub-events into one causal chain.

Later realization also does not erase the earlier expected-window failure. A binding decision issued after the declared deadline can recontextualize the earlier Non-Event, end its continuation, or create a new Event relation. It cannot make the prior non-realization within the earlier window never have occurred.

```text
later realization
≠ earlier bounded Non-Event retroactively erased
```

The source category nevertheless remains revisable. Finer evidence may show that the expected structure was partially realized, that separate expected windows existed, that several Non-Events should be distinguished, or that one claimed Non-Event was actually an Event Cluster plus an unsupported higher-level expectation. Preservation is an outcome to be earned, not an instruction to protect `Λ` from counterevidence.

#### Relational preservation rather than positive-event subtraction

The analyst should not determine the Non-Event by subtracting all positive Events from a temporal interval and naming the remainder. The relevant object is a relation:

```text
warranted expected structure
+
bounded realization condition
+
supported non-realization
+
praxis difference
```

Positive sub-events matter because they modify this relation. They may keep a process open, change the threshold, distribute responsibility, consume the time window, preserve an unresolved state, or create an alternative route. They are not merely “content inside an absence.” They are temporal and relational carriers of the bounded non-realization claim.

The coarse function can therefore remain stable while its internal architecture changes. One review cycle may fail through missing information; another through authority conflict; another through repeated deferral. Similar higher-level Non-Events do not imply identical internal structures. Conversely, similar internal sub-events do not imply the same expected structure or the same `Λ` object.

<a id="23-7-delay-structure"></a>

### 23.7 Delay Structure

Delay is a temporal relation between an expected or prepared continuation and its later realization, continued non-realization, or changed completion condition. It is not simply elapsed time. A long interval may be ordinary processing; a short interval may be a decisive delay where a narrow window closes. The source claim must specify what was delayed, relative to which timing condition, through which supported mechanism, and with what praxis-relevant effect.

#### C23-DELAY-01 — source-supported delay mechanisms, roles, thresholds, and dependencies

A Delay Structure may be decomposed through one or more of the following source-supported mechanisms:

- repeated postponement;
- queue or backlog;
- changing responsibility or jurisdiction;
- information bottleneck;
- resource unavailability;
- unmet quorum, approval, or readiness threshold;
- external dependency;
- rule-bound waiting period;
- missing decision capacity;
- strategic waiting, but only where independently supported;
- feedback or review loop that reopens an earlier phase.

The mechanism must be connected to roles, dependencies, and thresholds. For example:

```yaml
delay_candidate_R23:
  delayed_structure: binding review decision
  timing_condition: stated follow-up deadline
  mechanisms:
    - missing-information threshold prevented a first vote
    - responsibility transfer delayed access to the complete file
    - second meeting lacked the authority configuration required for binding closure
  dependencies:
    - information completion before authorized vote
    - assigned decision role before operative disposition
  supported_effects:
    - unresolved objection persisted
    - implementation coordination remained provisional
```

This map does not establish a total causal explanation. It identifies supported relations within the same bounded temporal object. Several mechanisms may operate concurrently, sequentially, or as rival explanations. One may be constitutive of the delay, another merely associated, and another underdetermined.

Delay also requires a comparison condition. The analyst must know enough about the expected timing, completion criterion, or readiness sequence to say that realization occurred later than warranted or remained unrealized. Metric duration alone is insufficient. The relevant difference may be procedural, relational, or threshold-based rather than a fixed number of days.

A changed deadline or suspended process can alter the Delay claim. If an authorized rule validly extends the window before expiry, the initial date may cease to govern. If the extension itself lacked authority or arrived after the expected window closed, the earlier delay may remain relevant. Chapter 23 records these temporal relations without deciding legal validity or legitimacy.

#### C23-INTENT-01 — delay does not prove intentional obstruction, motive, guilt, or person property

The internal structure of delay may be intentional, emergent, distributed, accidental, capacity-bound, rule-bound, or underdetermined. Duration, repetition, role transfer, or asymmetric benefit cannot alone prove motive.

```text
delay
≠ intentional obstruction automatically

repetition
≠ deliberate avoidance automatically

role transfer
≠ guilt automatically

structural benefit
≠ person motive automatically
```

Chapter 23 may state that a role repeatedly requested additional information, that a transfer predictably consumed the remaining window, or that one position benefited from continued non-resolution. It may not convert those structural findings into claims that a person intended obstruction, possessed an avoidant character, acted in bad faith, or deserved sanction.

An intention claim would require independent sources and a different evidential burden. Even where documentary evidence supports strategic timing, the claim remains occurrence-specific and bounded to the declared act or practice. It does not become a person type. Structural reconstruction can identify the operation of waiting without diagnosing the waiter.

This boundary also protects causal calibration. A delay may arise from interacting dependencies rather than one actor's choice. A queue, incomplete information, dispersed authority, and mismatched meeting schedules can jointly produce non-realization. Assigning intention because the outcome was stable would replace relational decomposition with retrospective person explanation.

<a id="23-8-repeated-non-decision"></a>

### 23.8 Repeated Non-Decision

Repeated Non-Decision is a candidate internal form in which several decision opportunities, procedural cycles, or closure attempts fail to produce the expected binding occurrence. Repetition matters only where the occurrences remain related to the same bounded question or where several distinct non-decisions are explicitly distinguished. Counting meetings or missed dates is not sufficient.

#### C23-NONDECIDE-01 — opening repeated non-decision without preselecting its final category

A repeated non-decision may contain:

- deferred votes;
- failed quorum or missing authority conditions;
- recurring reframing of the issue;
- reassignment between roles, committees, or offices;
- information requests that reopen collection or review;
- provisional closure without binding resolution;
- repeated expiry or replacement of decision windows;
- communications that preserve procedural continuation without disposition;
- absence of the required binding occurrence.

For R-23, the finer reconstruction may remain open among several category candidates:

```text
Candidate A:
one continuing Non-Event with several internal deferral phases

Candidate B:
several bounded Non-Events, each tied to a distinct decision window

Candidate C:
an Event Cluster of meetings, transfers, and requests
plus one higher-level Non-Event

Candidate D:
a broader temporal object whose continuation already requires Path classification
```

WP2 does not select among these candidates merely because repetition is present. The decision depends on reference continuity, expectation windows, local completion conditions, relation among the repetitions, and the scope of the source claim. If each meeting carried an independent duty to decide and each window closed separately, several Non-Events may be appropriate. If all cycles remained phases of one open procedure with one final deadline, one Extended Non-Event may be more accurate. If the analyst must form a broader realized sequence with branch and continuation structure, Chapter 24 or PATH ownership becomes relevant.

Reframing also requires care. A repeated shift in how the issue is stated may defer decision while preserving the underlying object, or it may materially replace the question and terminate the original expectation. A responsibility transfer can continue the same process, create a new decision window, or break reference continuity. These are category pressures, not automatic classifications.

#### C23-BIND-01 — absent binding remains relational and occurrence-specific

The expected decision in R-23 is binding only where the declared Frame specifies an occurrence capable of closing, authorizing, committing, or otherwise making the disposition operative. The absence of such an occurrence can be central to the Non-Event without becoming a claim about a person's identity, maturity, reliability, courage, or moral worth.

```text
binding occurrence absent
≠ person incapable of commitment

repeated non-decision
≠ indecisive person type

unresolved responsibility relation
≠ moral rank
```

The finer analysis must identify:

- which role or configuration possessed binding capacity;
- which preconditions activated that capacity;
- what occurrence would have counted as binding;
- whether provisional acts occurred without final closure;
- how the absence affected continuation, alternatives, exposure, or cost;
- which uncertainties remain.

A committee may deliberate extensively without producing the authorized vote. An office may issue guidance that lacks operative force. A chair may close discussion without resolving the admitted objection. These positive acts can be important while the binding occurrence remains absent.

The binding boundary also prevents Chapter 23 from importing the full operator-occurrence analysis of Chapter 21. WP2 may identify the absence of a required `Ψ`-typed occurrence as part of the temporal source object. It does not decompose `Ψ`, type a person through Ψ, or infer a general capacity for self-binding. The claim remains relational, procedural, occurrence-specific, and source-bound.

#### WP2 completion boundary

Sections 23.5–23.8 now establish:

- conjunctive Non-Event source entry through expected structure, Expectation Frame, bounded window, supported non-realization, praxis difference, and Source Ceiling;
- independent expectation testing and the possibility that the `Λ` claim fails;
- strict separation of missing records, unknown phases, mere absence, and structured non-occurrence;
- preservation of a higher-level Non-Event through positive sub-events that produce, stabilize, defer, fragment, or fail to overcome non-realization;
- later realization without retroactive erasure of an earlier bounded Non-Event;
- Delay Structure through supported mechanisms, roles, thresholds, dependencies, and comparison conditions;
- the prohibition against deriving intentional obstruction, motive, guilt, or person property from delay, repetition, transfer, or benefit alone;
- repeated non-decision through deferrals, authority or quorum conditions, reframing, role shifts, reopened information collection, provisional closure, and absent binding occurrence;
- occurrence-specific binding analysis without person typing or moral rank;
- open classification among one continuing Non-Event, several Non-Events, Event Cluster plus higher-level Non-Event, or a broader object requiring Chapter-24/PATH classification.

WP2 does not yet establish the complete internal temporal order, overlap and multiple-clock representation, temporal granularity drift result, Event/Non-Event confusion audit, final category effect, prior-claim disposition, canonical Output Class, Failure, Mandatory Stop, Non-Capture, the seventeen-field temporal Record view, Chapter-24 handoff, or Chapter-23 lock.

```text
WP2 complete
≠ Chapter 23 complete
≠ every positive process belongs to one Non-Event
≠ expectation automatically warranted
≠ delay proves intention
≠ repeated non-decision fixes the source category
≠ final Output Class selected
≠ Chapter 23 provisionally locked
≠ SUB provisionally locked
```

The Chapter-23 result remains `admissible_but_provisional`. The next controlled step is WP3, §§23.9–23.11: Internal Temporal Order, Temporal Granularity Drift, Event/Non-Event Confusion, result-axis completion, Failure, Mandatory Stop, Non-Capture, the temporal Record view, and the Chapter-24 handoff.

<a id="23-9-internal-temporal-order"></a>

### 23.9 Internal Temporal Order

A temporal decomposition is not completed by replacing one coarse date with many finer timestamps. Chapter 23 must reconstruct the relations through which phases, sub-events, delays, thresholds, interruptions, reopenings, and completion conditions belong to the same source object. The relevant order may be linear, partially ordered, overlapping, parallel, interrupted, recursive, or underdetermined. Its validity depends on source support and on whether the additional relation changes the warranted reconstruction of the Event or Non-Event.

#### C23-ORDER-01 — temporal order is relational, not a timestamp list

A timestamp list records positions. An internal temporal order explains how those positions are connected. At minimum, the reconstruction should distinguish where supported:

- enabling preconditions;
- initiating occurrences;
- dependent phases;
- threshold crossings;
- waiting intervals;
- parallel work;
- interruptions and resumptions;
- provisional completion;
- operative completion;
- reopening;
- irreversible partial transitions.

For R-23, the following list is insufficient:

```text
3 March — first meeting
7 March — information request
18 March — responsibility transfer
2 April — second meeting
15 April — deadline expiry
```

The praxeologically relevant structure is relational:

```text
first meeting
→ readiness threshold declared unmet

information request
→ required input remains outstanding

responsibility transfer
→ access route and authorized role change

second meeting
→ deliberation continues without binding threshold crossing

deadline expiry
→ tested decision window closes without operative disposition
```

The arrows do not claim universal causality. They state supported temporal dependencies inside the bounded object. Where one relation is uncertain, it must be marked as uncertain rather than silently supplied by chronological proximity.

This distinction also prevents a frequent source error. Consecutive records can describe parallel activities, duplicated reporting, or different views of the same occurrence. A later timestamp does not prove that the later item depended on the earlier one. Conversely, an undated rule or standing assignment may be temporally prior as a condition even when its document was retrieved later.

Internal order therefore requires both sequence and relation. Sequence answers what was earlier or later. Relation answers why the ordering matters for the source category, boundary, expectation, threshold, completion, or Source-Function Effect.

#### C23-OVERLAP-01 — overlap, partial order, parallelism, thresholds, interruptions, and multiple clocks remain visible

Many temporal objects cannot be represented responsibly as one line. Two phases may overlap. Several roles may work in parallel. One phase may depend on completion of only part of another. A formal deadline may close while practical consequences begin later. A procedure may be interrupted without losing reference continuity, or it may restart as a materially new occurrence.

Chapter 23 therefore permits a partial-order representation:

```text
information collection ───────────────┐
                                      ├→ readiness threshold
responsibility reassignment ──────────┘

readiness threshold
→ authorized deliberation
→ binding vote or continued non-realization
```

This map preserves dependencies without inventing a total order between every local act. Where the sources show only that two phases were completed before a threshold, their mutual ordering should remain unspecified.

Several clocks may also structure the same source object:

```text
formal clock:
agenda date → deadline → formal expiry

interaction clock:
first meeting → follow-up exchange → second meeting

information clock:
request → partial receipt → completeness determination

authority clock:
assigned role → transfer → authorized decision configuration

consequence clock:
continued provisional implementation → accumulated coordination cost
```

Evidence for one clock does not automatically fill another. Precise meeting dates do not establish when information became practically usable. A formal role transfer date does not prove immediate access. A deadline does not identify the exact moment at which consequences became irreversible. Each temporal claim needs its own source support.

Overlap may alter the source category. If information collection and deliberation proceed concurrently under one completion relation, the source may remain one Extended Event or one Extended Non-Event. If independent decision windows open and close separately, several Events or Non-Events may be warranted. If the analyst must connect a realized sequence of transitions, branches, and changed continuation possibilities into a broader historical object, Chapter 24 or PATH becomes the proper owner.

Interruption and resumption require the same discipline. Resumption preserves the earlier source object only where reference, operative question, relevant roles or succession rules, and completion conditions remain sufficiently continuous. A later activity with the same label may instead be a new Event, a new Non-Event window, or part of a newly composed Path.

<a id="23-10-temporal-granularity-drift"></a>

### 23.10 Temporal Granularity Drift

Temporal granularity is productive only where the finer distinction changes a warranted praxis reconstruction. Smaller units, more records, or a denser chronology possess no truth priority. A decomposition can become formally elaborate while remaining resolution-neutral, underdetermined, or below the Praxeological Relevance Floor.

#### C23-DRIFT-01 — finer temporal distinctions without changed reconstruction are drift or bounded no-gain pressure

A finer distinction has temporal purchase where it changes at least one supported claim about:

- the source boundary;
- Event or Non-Event category;
- internal phase relation;
- relevant threshold;
- role or authority configuration;
- expectation window;
- completion or non-completion;
- delay mechanism;
- transition structure;
- cost, exposure, access, or alternative;
- Source-Function Effect;
- Loss or uncertainty.

If the new detail changes none of these, the result should not be presented as a stronger decomposition merely because it is more precise in clock time.

```text
minute-level chronology
+ unchanged Event boundary
+ unchanged phase relation
+ unchanged expectation or completion claim
+ no new praxis difference
→ temporal granularity drift or bounded no-gain pressure
```

The local result may later map to `resolution_neutral`, `claim_reduction_required`, `mandatory_stop`, `failed_transformation`, or `non_capture`, depending on why the additional distinction lacks value. Chapter 23 does not redefine the complete resolution taxonomy owned by Chapter 25.

Temporal drift can arise through at least four patterns:

1. **instrument drift** — the recording system supplies smaller intervals than the praxis claim requires;
2. **phase proliferation** — one functionally continuous phase is divided into many named microphases without changed relations;
3. **duplicate-event drift** — several records of one occurrence are counted as several Events;
4. **chronology escape** — unresolved category or source problems are avoided by adding more dates.

No amount of timestamp density compensates for an unsupported Expectation Frame, missing source identity, invented completion relation, or absent traceable load. Formal precision cannot cross the Source Ceiling.

The stop question is therefore:

> Would one more temporal distinction alter a warranted reconstruction of the source object, its category, its internal relation, its praxis effects, or its Loss?

If not, Optional Stop is ordinarily appropriate. Mandatory Stop applies where continuation would require invention, source overreach, categorical substitution, person inference, or an undeclared change of operation.

<a id="23-11-event--non-event-confusion-results-and-completion"></a>

### 23.11 Event / Non-Event Confusion, Results, and Completion

Event and Non-Event decomposition produces recurrent categorical pressures. A positive sub-event can belong to a Non-Event structure. A delay can include many Events without itself being one Event. Missing information can prevent either category from being established. A later Event can terminate a continuing Non-Event without erasing the earlier bounded failure. These relations must remain explicit.

#### C23-CONFUSE-01 — Event, Non-Event, source absence, delay, and positive sub-events are not substitutes

The following substitutions are prohibited:

```text
positive activity
≠ expected structure realized

no positive record
≠ Non-Event established

delay
≠ Non-Event automatically

Non-Event
≠ empty interval

later completion
≠ earlier delay or bounded failure erased

many sub-events
≠ one Event Cluster automatically
```

For R-23, the first meeting, information request, responsibility transfer, second meeting, and deadline expiry may all be Events. They may jointly support one higher-level Non-Event. They may also support several separate Non-Events, or an Event Cluster plus one higher-level `Λ` occurrence. The correct result depends on the expectation windows, boundaries, phase relations, and reference continuity—not on a preference for positive or negative vocabulary.

Source absence remains a third condition. If the record does not show whether a vote occurred, neither Event nor Non-Event is established merely by choosing the more narratively coherent option. The proper result may remain underdetermined or Non-Capture.

A delay claim is similarly bounded. Delay requires a prepared or expected continuation and a supported temporal displacement. It may end in realization, continued non-realization, replacement of the expected structure, or termination of the relevant Frame. Delay is not automatically a Non-Event, because the delayed structure may still occur inside its operative window or the timing expectation may be unwarranted.

#### C23-TYPEFX-01 — four result axes remain separate

Chapter 23 preserves the Chapter-20 result architecture:

```text
local operation result
≠ category or Source-Function Effect
≠ prior source-claim disposition
≠ canonical Output Class
```

For the bounded R-23 reconstruction, one admissible provisional mapping may be:

```yaml
temporal_result_R23:
  local_operation_result: supported_relational_temporal_decomposition
  category_or_source_function_effect: internally_differentiated
  prior_source_claim_disposition: reduced
  canonical_output_class: admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

This mapping states that the temporal relations are sufficiently supported for a bounded decomposition; the coarse claim is internally differentiated; the prior formulation “one formal non-decision” is too compressed and must be reduced; and the current claim is admissible only within its declared boundary. It does not establish intention, causal sufficiency, legal validity, person responsibility, or an exhaustive category.

Other cases may produce `confirmed`, `refined`, `partially_preserved`, `rejected`, or `underdetermined` category effects. Those local effects are not Output Classes. The canonical class must still be selected from the closed inventory and justified through the full Admissibility Band.

#### Failure pressure

A temporal decomposition fails where it:

- lacks an independently identifiable source object;
- replaces the source with a timestamp list;
- inflates Events below the Relevance Floor;
- invents phases, thresholds, order, overlap, or completion;
- treats missing records as Non-Events;
- dissolves a supported Non-Event because positive sub-events were found;
- converts delay into intention, guilt, or person property;
- preserves an Event or Non-Event label after reference continuity has failed;
- silently composes independent Events into a new cluster;
- silently converts the object into a Path or Trajectory;
- silently projects a contextual target function;
- exceeds the Source or Claim Ceiling.

Failure is not identical with Mandatory Stop. A completed attempt can be classified as `failed_transformation` because its declared method did not preserve reference, category burden, or traceable relation. Mandatory Stop governs the point at which further continuation itself becomes inadmissible.

#### C23-STOP-01 — Mandatory Stop before invented temporal structure or undeclared operation change

Mandatory Stop applies where continuing Chapter-23 analysis would require:

- inventing an expected structure or Expectation Frame;
- inventing phases, sub-events, order, overlap, thresholds, or completion relations;
- treating unknown chronology as known;
- deriving intention, motive, guilt, legitimacy, sanction, or person type from delay or non-decision;
- preserving source identity after a material boundary or reference break;
- treating missing information as `Λ`;
- multiplying Event units without additional praxis purchase;
- silently switching to `COMPOSE`, PATH formation, Chapter-24 Path/Trajectory decomposition, or `PROJECT_AS`;
- exceeding the Praxeological Traceability Ceiling.

Stop is a positive control result. It protects the temporal object from fabricated precision and prevents a weak source claim from becoming more authoritative through formal detail.

#### C23-NC-01 — Non-Capture remains available without rescuing the coarse temporal claim

`non_capture` is appropriate where the question and source domain are legitimate but no stable finer reconstruction can responsibly be selected. This may occur where:

- rival Event boundaries remain equally supported;
- the Expectation Frame is plausible but insufficiently evidenced;
- positive sub-events are known but their relations are not;
- multiple clocks cannot be aligned without invention;
- interruption or resumption status remains underdetermined;
- the source could be one Extended Event, several Events, one Non-Event, several Non-Events, or a broader Path, with no responsible discriminator;
- every proposed finer map would destroy source identity or exceed the Source Ceiling.

```text
non_capture
≠ coarse Event claim confirmed
≠ coarse Non-Event claim confirmed
≠ missingness converted into Λ
≠ compromise chronology invented
```

Non-Capture does not state that all candidate maps are equally true. It states that the current transformation cannot responsibly capture the finer temporal object under the available Frame, sources, and claim burden.

#### Temporal Decomposition Record View

Chapter 23 uses the following seventeen-field operation-specific view:

```yaml
temporal_decomposition:
  source_temporal_object:
  source_category:
  source_boundary:
  source_function_or_expected_structure:
  expectation_basis:
  temporal_scope:
  source_granularity:
  target_granularity:
  decomposition_question:
  internal_phases_and_sub_events:
  internal_temporal_relations:
  delays_and_non_decisions:
  categorical_preservation_or_revision:
  temporal_drift_status:
  unresolved_structure:
  loss:
  admissibility_result:
```

For Non-Event claims, `expectation_basis` is mandatory. For Event claims, the field may record `not_applicable` with justification rather than being omitted silently. `source_boundary` must include beginning, completion or end condition, and relevant contextual margins. `internal_temporal_relations` must distinguish supported order from uncertain or merely possible order.

The view maps into the Shared Transformation Record rather than replacing it. Controlled extensions may carry competing boundary maps, multiple-clock relations, phase diagrams, or expectation-window details. Every extension remains non-authoritative, source-bound, and subordinate to the common Record, canonical Loss fields, governance controls, and Output Classes.

A complete Record cannot compensate for weak expectation support, missing relations, Type Integrity failure, Reference Drift, absent praxeological purchase, or traceability failure.

#### C23-HANDOFF-01 — protected completion and Chapter-24 handoff

The controlled continuation is:

```text
Chapter 23 provisional lock
→ Chapter 24 Preparation Gate
→ Chapter 24 — Decomposing Paths and Trajectories
```

Chapter 23 may hand Chapter 24:

- the same bounded temporal reference object;
- supported Event, Extended-Event, Event-Cluster, Non-Event, delay, or repeated-non-decision candidates;
- source boundaries and rival boundary constructions;
- internal phases, sub-events, partial orders, overlaps, interruptions, thresholds, and multiple clocks;
- expectation windows and supported non-realization relations;
- unresolved category and continuation questions;
- inherited and new Loss;
- the bounded Chapter-23 result axes and canonical Output Class.

Chapter 24 retains exclusive ownership of:

- opening a Path or Trajectory as the source object;
- distinguishing same Path from newly composed Path;
- branch, subpath, transition, and sedimentation reconstruction;
- Path versus Trajectory and Trajectory versus path-dependence tests;
- temporal reference continuity across broader realized development.

Chapter 25 retains the complete gain, neutrality, drift, and escape taxonomy. Chapter 26 retains full operation-boundary adjudication. Chapter 27 retains the complete SUB limit architecture. Chapter 28 retains cases, Local Audit, and Part output mapping. RETYPE retains contextual target functions.

A temporal object discovered to require a broader Path does not authorize Chapter 23 to continue under a different label. It triggers a new testable claim and the appropriate owner.

<a id="chapter-23-completion-boundary"></a>

#### Chapter 23 completion boundary

Sections 23.9–23.11 now establish:

- relational internal temporal order rather than timestamp accumulation;
- overlap, partial order, parallelism, interruptions, thresholds, and multiple clocks;
- temporal granularity drift and bounded no-gain pressure;
- explicit Event/Non-Event/source-absence/delay/category-confusion boundaries;
- separation of local operation result, category or Source-Function Effect, prior-claim disposition, and canonical Output Class;
- Failure, Mandatory Stop, and Non-Capture without compensation or claim rescue;
- the seventeen-field Temporal Decomposition Record View and Shared Record mapping;
- protected handoff to Chapter 24 and later owners.

The integrated WP4 audit confirms the Chapter-23 Contract, all twenty-four Preparation-Audit controls, all twenty-four Pressure Duties, Event/Non-Event source identity, boundary and expectation discipline, `Λ` preservation, missing-information and intention boundaries, internal-order and multiple-clock integrity, temporal drift control, result-axis separation, and package consistency. The Chapter-23 result is:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

Chapter 23 is therefore **provisionally locked**. The class names the chapter-level methodological result under the still-open SUB Part and later corpus-wide audits. It does not capture every temporal object, validate every Event boundary or expectation, convert uncertainty into chronology, establish intention or causality, complete Path/Trajectory decomposition, or increase claim, application, governance, or person-level authority.

All twenty-four Chapter-23 Pressure Duties occur exactly once in the canonical prose. No standalone Case, Transformation Record, Smoke Record, appendix expansion, schema artifact, new operation, Rule, Output Class, audit stage, temporal-object enum, clock score, intention field interpreted as fact, person type, or authority mechanism has been produced.

```text
Chapter 23 provisionally locked
≠ every temporal object captured
≠ finer timestamps possess truth priority
≠ Non-Event inferred from missing information
≠ intention or person property established
≠ Path or Trajectory ownership absorbed
≠ SUB provisionally locked
```

Reopening requires a concrete conflict involving PMS Base, Structure or Contract ownership, temporal source identity, Event/Non-Event category, boundary or completion relation, expectation or non-realization support, `Λ` preservation, source absence, delay/intention separation, repeated non-decision, internal order, multiple clocks, temporal drift, result axes, Failure/Stop/Non-Capture, a Pressure Duty, a Reference or Formal-Model binding, or a later integrated dependency. Preference for denser timestamps, more Event units, stronger causal or intentional language, a closed temporal taxonomy, automatic chronology, person typing, or premature Path/Trajectory/RETYPE claims is insufficient.

<a id="chapter-24-decomposing-paths-and-trajectories"></a>

## 24. Decomposing Paths and Trajectories

Chapter 24 applies `DECOMPOSE` to analytical objects that were already formed through PATH. Its source is not an unstructured chronology and not a loose collection of Events. It is an independently warranted Path, Trajectory, bounded subpath, Path phase, transition claim, branch cluster, turning-point claim, or Path-Dependence claim whose historical reference, temporal boundary, selection rule, formation rule, and coarser function were previously declared.

The chapter therefore begins from a formed temporal object carrying inherited compression. The source Path may already have selected some configurations as relevant, ordered transitions, retained particular Non-Events and alternatives, compressed parallel developments, and excluded local detail. Finer reconstruction can reopen those commitments. It cannot treat them as if they had never shaped the source object.

Sections 24.1–24.4 establish PATH-produced source entry, bounded decomposition reasons, original selection and formation lineage, Same-Reference pressure, subpaths, non-linear internal relations, transition clusters, and the boundary between opening one formed Path and composing another. Sections 24.5–24.8 extend that map through source-traceable and dimension-bounded turning points, historically available branches, source-bounded counterfactuals, internal Frame changes, reference and functional continuity, competing continuations, unequal accessibility, and preservation of reversals, interruption, parallelism, repair, and counter-trends. Sections 24.9–24.12 complete the architecture through inherited and current Loss separation, irrecoverable compression, non-invertibility, compression debt, bounded Path-Dependence load, Same-Path versus rival-PATH adjudication, four result axes, Failure, Mandatory Stop, Non-Capture, the nineteen-field Record view, and the Chapter-25 handoff.

The integrated Chapter-24 result is therefore a complete **Path/Trajectory `DECOMPOSE` procedure for already formed PATH objects**. It keeps the original selection and formation commitments visible, reconstructs finer internal courses without treating fragments as subpaths, preserves historical availability and non-linearity, separates recovered evidence from inverse recovery, and routes materially new selection or periodization to a separate `COMPOSE` claim. It does not privilege one periodization universally, turn Path Dependence into a substance or score, infer prediction or recommendation from competing continuations, recover irrecoverable history, assign a contextual target function, or increase claim, application, governance, or person-level authority.

```text
formed PATH object
+
explicit source reference, boundary, category, selection, formation, and coarse function
+
source-supported finer subpaths and transition relations
+
Same-Reference and operation-boundary test
→
Path/Trajectory DECOMPOSE candidate
```

<a id="24-1-path-objects-as-sub-objects"></a>

### 24.1 PATH Objects as SUB Objects

A PATH-produced object becomes a SUB source object only after its Path-side formation is independently warranted. The analyst must be able to identify what was composed, how it was ordered, which relations made the composition more than chronology, what function the resulting Path or Trajectory performed in the coarse reconstruction, and which Loss accompanied that formation.

Chapter 24 does not redefine Path or Trajectory. It inherits the canonical distinctions:

```text
sequence
≠ Path
≠ Trajectory

Trajectory
≠ Path Dependence
```

It adds a new operational question: which finer structures can be reconstructed inside the same formed object without replacing its historical referent or silently changing the operation?

#### C24-SOURCE-01 — independently warranted PATH-produced source entry

A Path or Trajectory may enter Chapter-24 decomposition only where the source declaration identifies at least:

- the historical reference;
- the source category under test;
- the governing Frame;
- the beginning, endpoint, or open temporal boundary;
- the coarser Path or Trajectory function;
- the original selection rule;
- the original ordering and formation rule;
- the relevant Non-Events, alternatives, and transition commitments;
- the inherited `COMPOSE` Loss;
- the finer-resolution question.

A bounded entry for the R-24 review-governance trajectory may be stated as:

```yaml
path_decomposition_entry:
  reference: review-governance development R-24
  source_category: provisional Trajectory
  frame: documented review and reconsideration process
  source_boundary: first admitted objection through bounded reconsideration outcome
  coarse_function: reconstruct how review access, decision authority, closure, and reconsideration became historically coordinated
  original_selection:
    - objection admission
    - repeated deferral
    - concentration of approval authority
    - operative closure
    - bounded reconsideration
  formation_rule: selected transitions are linked by the changing practical route from objection entry to closure and possible reopening
  inherited_compose_loss:
    compressed:
      - local intermediate configurations
      - parallel administrative activity
    excluded:
      - unrelated governance processes
    uncertain:
      - exact relation between several deferrals
    irrecoverable:
      - undocumented local alternatives at early stages
  decomposition_question: which subpaths and transition clusters carry or revise the coarse trajectory?
```

This declaration does not confirm that R-24 is a Trajectory. It makes the source category and the Path-side formation commitments testable. Finer reconstruction may preserve the Trajectory claim, reduce it to a Path, reveal competing Path constructions, or leave the category underdetermined.

The entry requirement blocks several substitutions:

```text
many dated records
≠ PATH-produced source object

shared topic across time
≠ same Path

same endpoint
≠ same historical development

coarse trajectory label
≠ warranted formation lineage
```

A set of Events handed forward from Chapter 23 does not become a Path merely because they are ordered. If the analyst newly selects and relates them into a realized development, that act belongs to `COMPOSE`. Chapter 24 begins only after a Path-side source object exists.

#### C24-SELECTION-01 — formation lineage remains visible

Every finer map must preserve the original selection and formation rule as an explicit object of audit. The analyst must distinguish:

1. source elements already constitutive of the coarse Path;
2. finer detail recovered inside those elements;
3. newly sourced material that may revise the coarse reconstruction;
4. new analytical selections made during the current decomposition;
5. material that remains excluded, uncertain, or irrecoverable.

A new source can legitimately reveal that an apparently single deferral contained several role transfers or that a coarse closure transition included a notice failure and later repair. It cannot retroactively erase the fact that the original Path was formed without these distinctions. The finer reconstruction must state whether it is clarifying an existing selected transition or changing which transitions constitute the object.

```text
new detail inside an inherited selection
≠ new selection automatically

materially new selection or periodization
≠ same DECOMPOSE claim automatically
```

Formation lineage prevents a later, richer account from presenting itself as the untouched history that was always fully present. It also allows the coarse Path claim to be revised without pretending that `DECOMPOSE` has inverted the original `COMPOSE`.

<a id="24-2-why-decompose-a-path"></a>

### 24.2 Why Decompose a Path?

A Path should be decomposed only where the existing compression is insufficient for a bounded praxeological claim. More chronology is not by itself a reason. The decomposition must identify what warranted reconstruction may change if finer subpaths, intermediate configurations, transition clusters, or internal relations become visible.

Legitimate pressure may arise where:

- one coarse transition hides several different mechanisms;
- parallel subpaths carry different roles, costs, or temporal speeds;
- the claimed Path function appears to lack traceable internal support;
- a linear narrative suppresses interruptions, reversals, or competing developments;
- a coarse Trajectory claim may depend on only one local dimension;
- inherited compression obscures relevant Non-Events or alternatives;
- a rival periodization pressures Same-Reference continuity;
- a countercase weakens the coarse Path or Trajectory claim.

#### C24-REASON-01 — decomposition reason and expected PraxisPurchase

Before opening the Path, the analyst must state:

- the insufficiency of the current compression;
- the finer distinction expected;
- the praxis-relevant claim that may be confirmed, refined, reduced, rejected, or left underdetermined;
- the evidence required;
- the condition under which no additional gain would be established.

For R-24, the coarse trajectory may state that review governance moved from admission through repeated delay into concentrated approval and closure. A bounded decomposition reason would not be “more records are available.” It may instead be:

> The coarse trajectory treats access, decision authority, and closure as one development. Finer reconstruction is required to determine whether these were one sequential Path, several partially parallel subpaths, or a later composition that conceals different temporal and burden structures.

The expected difference may concern:

- whether objection access remained open while decision authority narrowed;
- whether deferral belonged to the same transition as authority concentration;
- whether closure and reconsideration formed one continuation or two differently paced subpaths;
- whether the coarse direction claim survives internal counter-movements;
- whether the source is still one Trajectory rather than a looser Path.

The no-gain condition must also be explicit. If finer timestamps merely subdivide already known meetings without changing any transition, role, threshold, Non-Event, alternative, cost, Path relation, source category, or Loss, the result pressures `resolution_neutral` or later drift analysis rather than a stronger Path claim.

```text
more chronology
≠ more Path structure

more detail
≠ stronger Trajectory claim

finer resolution
≠ higher truth
```

The decomposition reason also limits scope. An analyst testing how approval authority became concentrated cannot absorb the entire institutional history merely because earlier policies provide context. Context may be necessary for traceability while remaining outside the source Path boundary.

<a id="24-3-subpaths"></a>

### 24.3 Subpaths

A subpath is a bounded internal course whose configurations and transitions remain reconstructibly related to the same coarse Path or Trajectory. It is not any temporally contiguous fragment and not any thematic subset. A valid subpath possesses its own local reference, internal transitions, and a stated relation to the source object's coarser function.

For R-24, three candidate subpaths may initially be distinguished:

```text
Subpath A — admission and access
objection submitted
→ admitted into review
→ access conditions modified

Subpath B — decision authority and non-decision
review assigned
→ information requested
→ decision deferred
→ approval authority concentrated

Subpath C — closure and reconsideration
operative closure communicated
→ reconsideration window opened
→ bounded reopening accepted, rejected, or expired
```

These candidates are not yet confirmed. Their boundaries and relations remain source-dependent. The purpose of the map is to test whether the coarse R-24 trajectory was carried by one serial development or by several internal courses that overlapped, constrained, or counteracted one another.

#### C24-SUBPATH-01 — source-related subpath versus arbitrary fragment

A candidate subpath must identify:

- its local source and endpoint;
- its internal configurations and transitions;
- the bounded praxis function it carries;
- its relation to the coarse Path;
- the evidence supporting its boundary;
- its temporal and relational overlap with other subpaths;
- what would make it a fragment rather than a subpath.

An arbitrary slice such as “all activity in March” is not a subpath unless that interval has a source-supported relation to the Path function. A subset such as “all emails” is not a subpath merely because the materials share a carrier. Conversely, a non-contiguous set of role transfers may form one subpath if they carry the same continuing authority relation across several coarse transitions.

```text
temporal segment
≠ subpath automatically

shared medium
≠ subpath automatically

source-related internal course
≠ arbitrary fragment
```

The distinction is functional and relational, not merely spatial or chronological. A subpath can be institutionally distributed, interrupted, or carried by different roles while remaining one bounded internal course.

#### C24-COHERENCE-01 — relation to the same coarse Path remains reconstructible

Subpaths must not replace the source Path with a bag of internal courses. Their relation to the same coarse object must remain visible. The analysis must explain whether a subpath:

- carries a constitutive part of the coarse Path function;
- modifies another subpath;
- delays or blocks another subpath;
- compensates for a local failure;
- creates a counter-direction;
- preserves an alternative;
- changes the accessibility or cost of continuation;
- reveals that the coarse Path claim requires revision.

For example, the admission subpath may remain open while the authority subpath becomes more concentrated. The closure subpath may complete formally while reconsideration remains practically inaccessible. These differences do not automatically destroy the coarse Path. They show that its internal coherence may depend on unequal timing and partially divergent functions.

If the subpaths cannot be related to one historical reference and one tested coarse function, the source object may fragment or the analysis may be constructing several independent Paths. WP1 does not decide the final category. It requires the analyst to expose the pressure rather than preserve one Path by label alone.

#### C24-ORDER-01 — internal order without forced linearity

Subpaths may stand in several relations:

```text
sequential
parallel
partially overlapping
competing
mutually constraining
interrupted and resumed
differently paced
locally reversible
```

The finer map must preserve whichever relations the sources support. It must not force all subpaths into one line merely because the coarse Path was narrated sequentially.

R-24 may show, for example, that access remained formally available while decision authority narrowed in parallel. A closure communication may occur before practical reconsideration access becomes usable. A repair route may reverse one local exclusion while leaving the broader approval structure unchanged. These are not narrative complications to be flattened; they are candidate internal relations whose relevance must be tested.

A partial order is acceptable where exact sequencing is unknown but dependency is supported. The analyst may know that information had to be available before a vote without knowing the exact order of several preparatory exchanges. Unsupported precision would reduce traceability rather than improve it.

<a id="24-4-transition-clusters"></a>

### 24.4 Transition Clusters

A coarse Path often contains transitions such as “review opened,” “authority concentrated,” or “closure became operative.” These labels may compress several intermediate configurations and mutually dependent changes. A transition cluster opens that coarse relation without automatically converting every internal occurrence into an independent Path segment.

A transition cluster must remain tied to one coarse source transition and explain how the source configuration became the target configuration. It may contain:

- positive Events;
- Non-Events;
- role or authority transfers;
- information thresholds;
- overlapping local transitions;
- partial decisions;
- temporary reversals;
- repair actions;
- changes in access, burden, or alternatives.

#### C24-TRANSCLUSTER-01 — intermediate configurations and transition relations

For every transition cluster, the analyst must identify:

- the coarse source and target configurations;
- the intermediate configurations;
- the relations connecting them;
- supported order, overlap, dependency, or threshold structure;
- the local contribution to the coarse transition;
- uncertainty and rival cluster maps;
- the effect on the coarse Path function.

The R-24 transition from repeated deferral to concentrated approval authority might be opened as:

```text
configuration 1:
review assigned across several roles

→ information request leaves readiness threshold unmet

configuration 2:
decision responsibility transferred

→ formal access remains, but authorized decision capacity narrows

configuration 3:
approval authority concentrated in one office
```

This cluster does not yet prove that the information request caused the authority concentration or that any role intended delay. It reconstructs a supported internal order and relation inside the coarse transition. Causal sufficiency, motive, blame, and person properties remain outside the claim.

A transition cluster also cannot be established by an expanded Event list alone. If meetings, messages, and records are added without showing how they changed the transition between configurations, the result is chronology inflation rather than decomposition.

#### C24-OPBOUND-01 — new Path formation and target function remain separate operations

WP1 must stop where the finer reconstruction ceases to open the same source Path.

A separate `COMPOSE` claim is required where the analyst:

- selects materially different source configurations;
- introduces a beginning or endpoint that changes the historical object;
- forms a new Path from previously independent subpaths or transition clusters;
- adopts a materially different relevance or periodization rule;
- turns formerly excluded material into constitutive Path segments;
- creates a macro-development not carried by the source formation lineage.

A separate `PROJECT_AS` claim is required where the Path or Trajectory is assigned a contextual function in another Frame, such as functioning as an organizational attractor, governance Frame, or higher-level constraint.

```text
same formed Path opened under finer resolution
→ DECOMPOSE candidate

new selection, periodization, or macro-Path
→ COMPOSE candidate

Path assigned a bounded contextual function
→ PROJECT_AS candidate
```

Operation classification is not a stylistic choice. Each route creates a different testable claim with its own source declarations, Loss, counterpressure, and result. Retaining the R-24 label does not preserve `DECOMPOSE` after the selection or formation rule has materially changed.

#### WP1 completion boundary

WP1 establishes PATH-produced source entry, explicit formation lineage, a bounded decomposition reason, Same-Reference pressure, subpath versus fragment, internal subpath relations, transition clusters, intermediate configurations, and the `DECOMPOSE`/`COMPOSE`/`PROJECT_AS` boundary.

It does not yet establish:

- turning-point reconstruction;
- branch statuses or source-bounded counterfactuals;
- internal Frame changes;
- competing continuations;
- non-linearity as an integrated coarse-direction test;
- inherited versus new Loss as the final Chapter-24 account;
- compression debt;
- decomposition of Path-Dependence load;
- rival-PATH adjudication;
- final result axes;
- Failure, Mandatory Stop, or Non-Capture;
- the nineteen-field Record view;
- Chapter-25 handoff;
- Chapter-24 provisional lock.

```text
WP1 complete
≠ Chapter 24 complete
≠ Same-Path automatically confirmed
≠ subpaths automatically constitutive
≠ transition cluster establishes causality
≠ rival periodization resolved
≠ final Output Class selected
≠ Chapter 24 provisionally locked
≠ SUB provisionally locked
```

<a id="24-5-turning-points"></a>

### 24.5 Turning Points

A turning point is a bounded transition or transition cluster whose occurrence materially alters the later Path space. It is not merely a memorable date, a narratively convenient milestone, or the moment that appears most important from the endpoint. A turning-point claim must remain anchored in historically traceable changes to alternatives, costs, asymmetries, bindings, action corridors, or continuation conditions.

The coarse R-24 trajectory contains several possible candidates:

```text
candidate T1
first objection admitted into review

candidate T2
repeated deferral becomes normalized

candidate T3
approval authority becomes concentrated

candidate T4
operative closure becomes effective

candidate T5
bounded reconsideration becomes available or expires
```

These candidates are not interchangeable. T1 may change access without changing decision authority. T2 may alter expectations and timing while leaving formal roles intact. T3 may change authority and cost distribution. T4 may close one continuation while opening another. T5 may repair, qualify, or merely formalize an already inaccessible alternative. The decomposition must therefore identify which component transitions carry the turning-point claim and which later structures depend on them.
